- This is how the pros in Japan defrost their tuna!!: Kind: captions Language: en Hello everyone, I'm Masa. When I defrost tuna, so much water comes out and it tastes kind of bland. Ever felt like this? Today, I'm going to show you a prolevel way to defrost tuna properly. This method comes from Kitani Suisan, a professional seafood wholesaler at Toyosu and Market in Japan. I'll be following the technique today. I'm going to compare two methods the professional way and just leaving the tuna out at room temperature. First, I need to make warm salt water at about 40° C. In Japan, most homes have water heaters that let you adjust the exact temperature, so it's easy to get 40° C water. Since the temperature drops a bit over time, I actually start with 42° C. Next, I add salt to make the water about 4% salt concentration. That's because tuna itself has around 4% salinity. So, matching it helps keep the balance. Now, give the tuna a quick rinse with water. This is just to brush off the ice around the tuna. Then put it in the warm salt water for about 3 minutes. By keeping the salt concentration the same as the tuna, I prevent water from being absorbed into the tuna and stop the umami flavors from leaking out. Here is the science behind it. If tuna stays in the temperature range of -5° C to -1° C for too long, its cells break down and the flavors escapes. Using warm water helps the tuna pass through that range quickly, keeping it delicious. Take the tuna out. And since there's still a bit salt on the surface, just give it a quick rinse. Then gently pat it dry with some paper towels. At this stage, the inside is still slightly frozen, so I will let it rest in the fridge for about 4 hours. Some people also use ice water at this point instead. Try to keep as much air out as possible. That's the key. Let's compare this with the tuna that was defrosted at room temperature. The properly defrosted tuna looks bright and appetizing. While the room temperature defrosted tuna has turned dark, you can also see the liquid cold drip. It contains the tuna's natural umami, nutrients, and blood. You can really tell when I pick it up. Properly defrosted tuna feels firm and there's no excess moisture. But the one defrosted at room temperature, it's super watery. And when you taste it, there's barely any tuna flavor left. All right, let's make some sushi. It's beautiful, isn't it? By the way, if you want to speed things up, instead of letting it rest in the fridge after the warm salt water step, you can place it in ice water. It will defrost faster that way. Since it's been frozen once, there's no need to worry about parasites like anisakis. But just a heads up, refreezing isn't a good idea. So be careful with that. Try out this method for yourself and see the difference. Thank you for watching until the end. If you enjoyed this video, don't forget to hit the like button and subscribe for more sushi tips. Bye-bye.

- 【Easy】How to Make Restaurant-Quality Sushi & Sashimi at Home!!: Kind: captions Language: en Today, I'm gonna turn this supermarket-bought fish&nbsp;&nbsp; into something that tastes like it&nbsp; came from a professional restaurant.&nbsp; If you're planning to enjoy sashimi or&nbsp; sushi at home, make sure to try this out! So, let's get started. Here’s what I have prepared today. And later&nbsp; on, I will also use some saltwater ice. Now, let’s sprinkle some salt on this salmon. Why do i do this? First, let me tell you why. Most of the fish sold at supermarkets&nbsp; are usually frozen and then thawed.&nbsp; As a result, the fish retains a lot of moisture,&nbsp;&nbsp; and if you eat it like this,&nbsp; it can taste bland and watery. By sprinkling the salt, the excess&nbsp; moisture is drawn out through osmosis.&nbsp; This tightens the flesh and&nbsp; concentrates the flavor,&nbsp;&nbsp; while also removing some of the fishy smell. This process is called 'Shio-jime' in&nbsp; Japan, which means tightening with salt. Let's actually do it. The amount of salt I’ll use this&nbsp; time is 1.5% of the fish’s weight.&nbsp; This is what the head chef&nbsp; of 'Sushi Hiroya' said that. It's important to sprinkle the salt as evenly as&nbsp;&nbsp; possible. Professional chefs&nbsp; don’t even measure the salt;&nbsp; they just know the right amount&nbsp; by eye. That’s craftsmanship! After sprinkling the salt, let the fish&nbsp; sit for about 20-30 minutes to dehydrate.&nbsp; If it’s winter, you can leave it&nbsp; at room temperature, but in summer,&nbsp;&nbsp; it’s better to keep it in the fridge. Look at that! You can see the excess&nbsp; moisture that’s been drawn out.&nbsp; And you can also see the moisture being&nbsp; drawn out from the surface of the salmon Next, I will wash this&nbsp; salmon with 1% saltwater ice.&nbsp; Did you think washing it with saltwater&nbsp; would make it salty? Don’t worry! What’s important here is to never use&nbsp; fresh water. If you use fresh water,&nbsp; it will rapidly strip away both the&nbsp; salt and the flavor from the fish,&nbsp;&nbsp; so be careful! Once it's been rinsed, dry it off with some paper towel. Now Look at this! there’s no more excess&nbsp; moisture and the flesh is firm It’s clear just by looking at it&nbsp; compared to before I salted it! Now, if you leave it in the fridge for&nbsp; another 30 minutes, it will taste even better.&nbsp; Even if you don’t finish it all&nbsp; today, it’ll keep for 2-3 days. Alright, let’s make some&nbsp; sashimi or sushi and try it! And Let's eat!! Itadakimasu!! First, Sashimi It’s not salty at all, there’s almost&nbsp;&nbsp; no fishy smell, and it tastes amazing!! It's as delicious as the salmon I get&nbsp;&nbsp; at a restaurant. Sushi is also good! It’s really simple, so&nbsp; definitely give it a try at home!

- Why Japan's 600-Year-Old Breakfast Still Works Better Than Ours: Kind: captions Language: en This isn't your usual toast and coffee breakfast. No, this is a format that's been refined over centuries here in Japan. It's called ichiju sansai, one soup, three sides, a balanced framework that's fueled generations with steady energy and calm focus. It looks simple, but once you see how intentionally it's designed, you'll never look at breakfast the same way again. Today, I'll show you how to make it, why it works, and how you can adapt it to your own mornings. I'm Dan Martin, chef, nutritionist, and half Japanese. Since moving back here, I've built a business, a family, and honestly feel sharper and more balanced than ever before. And I think a big part of that comes from starting most days with this exact blueprint. Hajimemashou. Before we cook, here's why this meal works so well. Your brain is an energy hog. About 1/5 of everything you eat fuels it. Think of it like this. One in every five bites goes straight to your brain. Now, when breakfast is just something refined like white bread, sugary cereal, pastries, your blood sugar spikes quickly and falls quickly, too. Your brain feels those swings and concentration drifts. This Japanese style fixes that. Carbs paired with protein, fiber, healthy fats, and fermentation. The rise is steadier and the energy and mental clarity last longer. Protein also supplies amino acids, the raw materials for neurotransmitters. Then you add fatty fish and you also get omega-3s, DHA for brain signaling and EPA for reducing inflammation. And because it's built on variety, grains, vegetables, pickles, fish, it naturally covers more nutrients than a breakfast based on one or two foods. It's less about one superfood and more about the balance between them. That's the power of design in food. Quick note, before we begin, this isn't a step-by-step recipe video. I want you to focus on what each component does and how they work together. The full steps are in the description and if you want a full step-by-step video, tell me in the comments. All right, let's build it starting with the rice since it takes the longest. I'm using Zakkokumai, a Japanese mixed grain rice. A mix of grains and colors, each adding its own fiber, minerals, and phytonutrients. More plant diversity means broader nutrient coverage and a steadier start. You'll often see meals like this served with plain rice, but this version's got more bang for your buck. Next, the protein. Today, I'm grilling mackerel, simple, affordable, and one of the richest natural sources of omega-3s. Rich, oily, and full of flavor. In some countries, it's labeled as a trash fish, but it's one of the most underrated sustainable fish out there. I salt it lightly and grill skin-side down for a few minutes. Then flip to crisp up the skin. Serve with grated daikon, but it's not just garnish. Raw daikon contains enzymes that help with digestion. For the miso soup, I start with a cold brew dashi made from kombu and dried shiitake. Gentle, balanced, and full of umami. Cold brewing keeps the flavor clean and the iodine levels lower, which helps if, like me, you drink this almost every day. Then I bring it up to a gentle simmer, add the mushrooms, they're beautiful this time of year, and let them cook just long enough to release that deep, earthy aroma. Now, off the heat, I whisk in miso so I don't destroy the probiotics and add silken tofu. Now, for the veggie side, I'm using mizuna and a little myoga, Japanese ginger. With a quick sesame dressing that's made from toasted sesame seeds, soy, a touch of syrup, and lemon. Mizuna's part of the brassica family, the same group as kale and mustard greens, rich in compounds that support circulation and brain health. It's nutty, peppery, and aromatic, perfect for this meal. And to finish, a few slices of cucumber nukazuke, pickled and fermented rice bran. Fermentation was one of Japan's main ways to preserve food before refrigeration, and nukazuke used the rice bran left from polishing grains. Crisp, tangy, and full of beneficial bacteria. Quick note, if you're enjoying this, I send a short weekly email with recipes and nutrition insights I don't always share on YouTube. The link's below if you'd like to join. Okay, back to it. Here's the framework this breakfast is built on. Balanced design for calm, lasting focus. First, a steady source of carbohydrates. Here, it's rice. Carbs supply glucose, your brain's preferred fuel. When they're paired with fiber and protein, the energy releases gradually. Next, fermented or fibrous foods, miso, pickles, natto, they nourish your gut microbes, which in turn aid digestion, immunity, and even brain function through the gut-brain axis. Then, quality protein. In this plate, the fish. Protein provides the building blocks for your muscles, enzymes, and hormones, and it helps you stay satisfied and mentally sharp through the morning. But it doesn't have to be fish. Eggs, tofu, tempeh, they're all quality protein sources, too. And your fats might come from olive oil, avocado, nuts, or seeds. Then come the healthy fats, in this case from the fish and the sesame seeds in the dressing. They support nutrient absorption and help maintain even energy levels. And finally, add colorful plants, vegetables, seaweeds, herbs, each bringing its own antioxidants and minerals that support circulation and overall vitality. That's the idea, balanced carbs, quality protein, good fats, color, and fermentation, harmony you can recreate anywhere. You see, once you understand the design, you can adapt it to any kitchen and really to any meal of the day. This format isn't just a breakfast design, it's a framework used across Japanese meals, each one balanced with the same sense of harmony. Now, a Western version might look like this. Whole grain toast for the carb, eggs for the protein, a drizzle of olive oil for the healthy fats, greens for color and micronutrients, and a spoonful of kimchi for the fermented food. Same principles, different form. Now, not everyone has time for a full spread, but the blueprint works anywhere. Since moving back to Japan, I've made this my default. Most mornings I don't cook the full spread, just rice, miso, a protein, maybe some natto, or some form of egg. Simple, quick, and steady because the point isn't perfection, it's balance practiced a little every morning. If you want to see how this framework looks in a 5-minute Western version, watch my brain-boosting breakfast bowl. I'll link it here. Or if you'd rather work on the habit side, check out my focus video. It's in the description. However you start, start with intention. I'll see you there.

- Mackerel: The Super fish You Need to Know: Kind: captions Language: en Mackerel is a fish that has been enjoyed for centuries. It is a popular choice for its rich savory flavor and versatility in the kitchen. Mackerel is also a nutritional powerhouse packed with essential vitamins, minerals, and healthy fats. From grilled fillets to smoky pates, mackerel offers a taste of the ocean with a world of health benefits. Let's dive in and explore what makes this fish so special. Mackerel belongs to a family of fish known for their sleek torpedo-shaped bodies and distinct stripes or spots. Found in both the Atlantic and Pacific oceans, these fish are known for their strong swimming abilities and play a crucial role in the marine ecosystem. Their abundance and relatively quick reproduction cycle make them a sustainable seafood choice, appealing to environmentally conscious consumers. But the real magic of mackerel lies in its nutritional profile. This superfish is an excellent source of protein, essential for building and repairing tissues in the body. It is also rich in B vitamins which are vital for energy production and maintaining a healthy nervous system. However, the true stars of the mackerel show are its high levels of omega-3 fatty acids and the essential trace mineral [Music] selenium. Omega-3 fatty acids are essential fats that our bodies cannot produce on their own. We must obtain them through our diet. These healthy fats play a crucial role in maintaining heart health by lowering blood pressure, reducing triglycerides, and decreasing the risk of blood clots. Omega-3s are also important for brain health, supporting cognitive function, and may even play a role in reducing the risk of depression. Selenium, another nutritional powerhouse found abundantly in mackerel, acts as an antioxidant in the body. Antioxidants protect ourselves from damage caused by free radicals, unstable molecules that can contribute to aging and disease. Selenium also plays a vital role in supporting a healthy immune system, helping our bodies fight off infections and stay healthy. The combination of omega-3 fatty acids and selenium in mackerel makes it a true superfish for overall health and well-being. However, not all mackerel are created equal. Understanding the differences between species, particularly Atlantic and King mackerel, is important for making informed choices about consumption. Atlantic mackerel and king mackerel are two popular types of mackerel, each with distinct characteristics. Atlantic mackerel, smaller in size, is generally considered a more sustainable choice. They have a milder flavor and are often canned or smoked. King mackerel, on the other hand, are larger and have a richer, more pronounced flavor. They are often grilled, baked, or pan fried. While both types of mackerel offer valuable nutrients, there is a key difference to consider. Mercury levels. Mercury is a heavy metal that can accumulate in fish, especially larger predatory fish like king mackerel. High levels of mercury consumption can be harmful to human health, particularly for pregnant women and young children. Atlantic mackerel, due to their smaller size and lower position on the food chain, generally contain lower levels of mercury compared to king mackerel. This makes Atlantic mackerel a safer and healthier choice, especially for those concerned about mercury exposure. Section four, smaller is better. Choosing your mackerel wisely. When it comes to mackerel, smaller is often better. Opting for Atlantic mackerel or other smaller species helps minimize mercury exposure while still reaping the nutritional benefits of this superfish. Look for mackerel that has been sustainably sourced, ensuring that fishing practices minimize environmental impact. Reading labels and understanding where your fish comes from can help you make informed choices. Don't hesitate to ask your fishmunger about the origin of the mackerel and their recommendations for the best options. Remember, making conscious choices about seafood consumption not only benefits our health, but also the health of our oceans. Section five, mackerel on your plate. Simple and delicious. Incorporating mackerel into your diet doesn't have to be complicated. Its versatility allows for a wide range of culinary creations from simple weekn night meals to elegant dinner parties. Grilled mackerel fillets brushed with olive oil, lemon juice, and herbs make for a flavorful and healthy meal. Flaked mackerel can be added to salads, sandwiches, or pasta dishes for a boost of protein and omega-3s. Smoked mackerel with its rich smoky flavor can be enjoyed on crackers, in dips, or as part of a shakuterie board. Get creative in the kitchen and explore the many delicious ways to enjoy this superfish. From its impressive nutritional value to its versatility in the kitchen, mackerel offers a world of benefits. By choosing smaller species and enjoying it in moderation, we can reap the rewards of this superfish while making responsible choices for our health and the environment. So, next time you're looking for a healthy and delicious protein source, consider mackerel, the superfish you need to know

- Will the ocean ever run out of fish? - Ayana Elizabeth Johnson and Jennifer Jacquet: Kind: captions Language: en Fish are in trouble. The cod population off Canada's East Coast collapsed in the 1990s, intense recreational and commercial fishing has decimated goliath grouper populations in South Florida, and most populations of tuna have plummeted by over 50%, with the Southern Atlantic bluefin on the verge of extinction. Those are just a couple of many examples. Overfishing is happening all over the world. How did this happen? When some people think of fishing, they imagine relaxing in a boat and patiently reeling in the day's catch. But modern industrial fishing, the kind that stocks our grocery shelves, looks more like warfare. In fact, the technologies they employ were developed for war. Radar, sonar, helicopters, and spotter planes are all used to guide factory ships towards dwindling schools of fish. Long lines with hundreds of hooks or huge nets round up massive amounts of fish, along with other species, like seabirds, turtles, and dolphins. And fish are hauled up onto giant boats, complete with onboard flash freezing and processing facilities. All of these technologies have enabled us to catch fish at greater depths and farther out at sea than ever before. And as the distance and depth of fishing have expanded, so has the variety of species we target. For example, the Patagonian toothfish neither sounds nor looks very appetizing. And fishermen ignored it until the late 1970s. Then it was rebranded and marketed to chefs in the U.S. as Chilean sea bass, despite the animal actually being a type of cod. Soon it was popping up in markets all over the world and is now a delicacy. Unfortunately, these deep water fish don't reproduce until they're at least ten years old, making them extremely vulnerable to overfishing when the young are caught before they've had the chance to spawn. Consumer taste and prices can also have harmful effects. For example, shark fin soup is considered such a delicacy in China and Vietnam that the fin has become the most profitable part of the shark. This leads many fishermen to fill their boats with fins leaving millions of dead sharks behind. The problems aren't unique to toothfish and sharks. Almost 31% of the world's fish populations are overfished, and another 58% are fished at the maximum sustainable level. Wild fish simply can't reproduce as fast as 7 billion people can eat them. Fishing also has impacts on broader ecosystems. Wild shrimp are typically caught by dragging nets the size of a football field along the ocean bottom, disrupting or destroying seafloor habitats. The catch is often as little as 5% shrimp. The rest is by-catch, unwanted animals that are thrown back dead. And coastal shrimp farming isn't much better. Mangroves are bulldozed to make room for shrimp farms, robbing coastal communities of storm protection and natural water filtration and depriving fish of key nursery habitats. So what does it look like to give fish a break and let them recover? Protection can take many forms. In national waters, governments can set limits about how, when, where, and how much fishing occurs, with restrictions on certain boats and equipment. Harmful practices, such as bottom trawling, can be banned altogether, and we can establish marine reserves closed to all fishing to help ecosystems restore themselves. There's also a role for consumer awareness and boycotts to reduce wasteful practices, like shark finning, and push fishing industries towards more sustainable practices. Past interventions have successfully helped depleted fish populations recover. There are many solutions. The best approach for each fishery must be considered based on science, respect for the local communities that rely on the ocean, and for fish as wild animals. And then the rules must be enforced. International collaboration is often needed, too, because fish don't care about our borders. We need to end overfishing. Ecosystems, food security, jobs, economies, and coastal cultures all depend on it.

- Overfishing: The worst and best fish you can eat: Kind: captions Language: en Seafood, in my opinion, is the best food ever. It’s healthy, it's fresh, it's delicious... Until I saw these pictures: Maybe you’ve also seen some recent documentaries on the fishing industry. If you didn’t, this is the gist: “We are at war with the oceans and if we win this&nbsp;war&nbsp;we’re going to lose it all because mankind is not able to live on this planet with a dead sea.” Well, I got terrified. What have I done with all of the fish and chips and sushi and shrimps and oysters that I’ve eaten all along?! Is there still any kind of seafood that I can eat without feeling like I'm responsible for destroying entire ecosystems? And which type should I just avoid in general? Well, it got so much messier than I thought it would. "Do you know where this [salmon] is from?" Let me break it down for you. After the whole debate about meat and dairy farming and its consequences, seafood has come under greater scrutiny. Global&nbsp;prodution&nbsp;of seafood has quadrupled over the past 50 years. While&nbsp;artisinal&nbsp;and&nbsp;subsistance&nbsp;fishing used to be the norm, industrial grade wild capture has dominated over the past decades. The UN estimates a third of all seafood might be overfished. We’re&nbsp;emptying the oceans at an alarming rate, and not only that – the way we fish endangers other marine animals and entire ecosystems. Seafood sustainability is a very touchy subject. Everybody I spoke to said: “You know how complicated seafood is.” But I found out there is some seafood you can eat with a relatively good conscience if you follow some rules of thumb. The first question you need to ask yourself sort if you're buying seafood is: Is this species endangered or not? For some it’s super clear-cut. "So&nbsp;if you can avoid bluefin tuna in sushi restaurants and other places, please do. And the second is another species&nbsp; most commonly eaten&nbsp;in sushi, which is unagi. There are many different species within that category, many different types of&nbsp;eel. But for them, for&nbsp;the vast majority of&nbsp;them, they are in bad straits. And I recommend you do not eat them." That’s Ryan Bigelow, who has been working for the database Seafood Watch for over 10 years. Greenpeace has a red list of fish – fish in the wild that are endangered – all the resources I mention you can find in the description box below. This is a quite a blanket list. If you want to dig into exactly which fish stock is endangered where, you can&nbsp;turn to data bases for more precise information. One of the best known is the Seafood Watch program. It scientifically assesses and lists thousands of&nbsp;species and their sustainability status. "Our recommendations are very, very specific to types of fisheries. How healthy is this population of this fish if you're trying to catch a&nbsp;tuna, how many of those tuna&nbsp;are&nbsp;left? Are you catching sharks or catching things you don't want? Are you impacting that natural environment? Are there rules in place to protect those species? There is quite a bit of difference depending on how and where it's caught." So&nbsp;let’s talk about how these fish are caught. Fishing methods using large, industrial grade gear in marine environments often cause the most damage. Number one is bottom trawling, where huge nets the height of buildings and the length of football fields scour the ocean floor, causing vast amounts of bycatch – catching and&nbsp;killing&nbsp;other, non-target fish and endangered animals. This often happens when fishing for bottom-dwelling fish like hake or flounder. It also harms&nbsp;sea beds. This is what the seabed looked like before – and after bottom trawling. Number two is longline fishing, which targets big species like tuna or swordfish. These lines can be miles long and have thousands of hooks, causing – again – bycatch. Depending on where you live, you can get information on fishing methods on the packaging. "So&nbsp;of course&nbsp;from a fishery sustainability point of view, we would absolutely benefit from having more selective gear." This is Minna Epps, who is responsible for the protection of marine and polar environments at the IUCN and has previously worked for the Marine Stewardship Council. More selective means targeting the species you want more accurately – like rod and reel fishing, or harpooning, or cast nets. You can also use special hooks in longlining called circle hooks - that don’t immediately kill non-targeted fish or other marine animals. This doesn’t mean you never can eat fish that’s caught with a long line – that’s what the detailed seafood guides are for – it’s just information that you can&nbsp;take into account when you want to eat big, wild-caught fish. So&nbsp;what about farmed fish – like this salmon? Catching wild fish used to be the dominant way to get to fish. But Aquaculture seafood production has recently overtaken wild catch, its growth accelerated mostly by farms in China, India and Indonesia. But farming big fish in open pens in marine environments has its own host of problems. Many farmed fish like salmon are mostly carnivores – meaning they need to be fed fish. Estimates vary, but around ten to twenty percent of all wild-caught fish may have been used to feed farmed seafood. On top of that, if the fish farms are not managed well, pesticides, antibiotics, food waste as well as fish excrement can poison the environment. The spread of diseases to and from wild populations, as well as parasites are also major problems. But omnivorous fish – like tilapia, carp, catfish or pangasius can, if managed well – be farmed more sustainably. They can eat plant-based feed and can be farmed in bigger ponds or lakes in a more natural environment. But sustainable management is still the exception in emerging and developing countries where aquaculture is concentrated. The most sustainable form of aquaculture is shellfish farming – like mussels, oysters or clams. They require little feed, filter the water and are very fast-growing. So&nbsp;if you want to be on the safe side, that’s the farmed seafood to go for. With one exception: Farmed shrimp and prawn – a lot of which contributes massively to environmental degradation, not to mention social injustice. "I know that a large percentage, if not most shrimp is red rated and the industry has some serious problems." One way, we are told, that we can make sure that the food we eat is not responsible for&nbsp;all of&nbsp;these problems – is to look out for certifications. There is the Marine Stewardship Council label – MSC – long seen as the gold standard of sustainable wild-caught seafood. Then there’s the aquaculture Stewardship Council label – ASC. Or the dolphin safe tuna label. Let’s have a look at the MSC. It is the most widely accepted certification, covering about 15 percent of the market. This one's particular interesting, so it has a MSC label and it even says it's a 100% sustainable. But it's caught with bottom-trawling. How does that go together? Basically. "We don't consider only the gear type to determine whether or not&nbsp;sustainability is possible, any fishery will have some level of impact." This is Michael&nbsp;Mariott, MSC program manager for Africa, Middle East and South Asia. "The question is whether or not that impact can be managed within tolerable limits." The MSC looks at three principles: Whether the targeted stock is overfished, what impact the fishing has on the wider ecosystem, and the strength of the fisheries management. But the MSC has seen criticism – for example for certifying fisheries with a high percentage of bycatch. "When these fisheries have bycatch, maybe they may need to report it, but they can just throw it overboard without anyone noticing it." This is Frank&nbsp;Wijen, who co-authored a study on the controversy surrounding the Marine Stewardship Council. "There have been observations that several adopters did not scrupulously comply with MSC arrangements." "We don't expect fisheries to have zero bycatch. I think that would be unrealistic for most, if not all, commercial fishing. But MSC certified fisheries are expected to provide evidence that they're reducing, that they&nbsp;are mitigating&nbsp;their bycatch impacts." So that means – if you have the MSC label, you can’t always be sure it’s really sustainable. But in more cases than not, it is better than a non-certified seafood. At least if you are in the North American or European region. There are many regional labels that have good standards – but also labels that are given by the industry, without any independent certification. So&nbsp;read up if you want to make sure they mean anything. Ok I know this a hell of a lot to process. Labels can be good, but they are not always. Food produced in aquaculture can be good but not always. And to be honest, a lot of this information is quite hard to come by if you're an average consumer. Especially if you’re not cooking from scratch. So&nbsp;here are some rules of thumb that the experts gave me. "If you want to minimize the impact or the pressure on the ocean it's also to diversify, it's to eat from a broader palette." "If you can be more adventurous in your seafood consumption, that's a good thing. Work some shellfish in your diet, try a sustainable source of tilapia or another farmed fish. There's a lot of other good seafood out there. We just have to expand our horizons." Eating smaller fish that repopulate faster like anchovies, sardines or small mackerels that are fished sustainably rather than only eating the bigger fish is a way to take some pressure off the bigger, popular species. "Just asking where your fish comes from. It actually shows that you care whether you're in a restaurant or in a shop." "When I go to a restaurant and they don't know, that's a red flag. They should know a little bit of information. If there is a good vegetarian option, go for that, if not, get the fish and do a little more research before you go out next time." "Do you know where [the salmon] is from? Is it raised in aquaculture?" So they didn’t know where the fish was from. They said the tuna may be bluefin tuna, so I stayed far away from that. I got something vegetarian. Very good! "Sometimes the unintended effect of using standards could be that we think that, oh, sustainability has been covered so I can eat as much as I like. But it surely should not be a sort of a free ticket towards consuming as much as you would like to consume." Some suggest that the only way to go is to eat no seafood at all. And that's a powerful option. But it's not viable for vast parts of the world’s population who rely on it for food security or for their income. The much bigger problem is the overconsumption of seafood in the global north. For those of us who are lucky enough to pick and choose – eating less seafood and caring more about where it comes from is a step in the right direction. So this whole story got so much more complicated than I thought it would. Did this help you decide what to eat? What else would you like to know about eating sustainably? Pleas let&nbsp;us&nbsp;know&nbsp;in&nbsp;the&nbsp;comments. We post videos like this every friday!

- The High-Tech Future of Sustainable Fishing: Kind: captions Language: en [♪ INTRO] Over the past couple hundred years, we’ve become a little bit&nbsp; too good at catching fish. As a result, we’ve completely&nbsp; transformed the oceans. A 2021 study estimated that we have reduced the mass of fish in the sea by 60% since 1800. But advances in technology don’t have&nbsp; to be exclusively bad news for fish. They can also help make&nbsp; fisheries more sustainable. A sustainable fishery means the&nbsp; fish population can be maintained, even if we’re catching a&nbsp; certain amount every year. This means fishers need to be&nbsp; selective about what they catch, scientists need to monitor the ecosystem,&nbsp; and everyone needs to play by the rules. And technology can help us do all of that. So here are three ways that new&nbsp; tech can be a fish’s best friend! When fishers are trying to catch one species, they often end up getting others along the way. This is called bycatch, and it makes up&nbsp; around 10% of all fish caught globally. It’s a big problem. You can’t limit the number of&nbsp; fish taken out of an ecosystem if you can’t control which fish are caught. The worst case scenario is that those unintended catches could be protected species. The key to reducing this is to&nbsp; make fishing tools more selective. But dragging a net through the water to capture one type of fish and not another&nbsp; is easier said than done. One promising solution is to actually install&nbsp; little illuminated exit signs in the nets. We know that different species of&nbsp; fish respond to light differently. It can signal food or danger,&nbsp; and the responses can vary depending on the light’s properties. The intensity, color, flashing rate, and even polarization of light can&nbsp; all affect fish behavior. Fishers have used lights to attract&nbsp; fish, but studies have found that carefully placed LEDs can also help&nbsp; fish find their way out of a net. A 2020 study placed LEDs on the areas of&nbsp; scallop nets where the mesh was wider, and designed to let the unintended catch out. In certain scenarios, like deeper water, the exit lights reduced&nbsp; bycatch by between 25 and 47%! And a 2015 study found that lights on the line of a shrimp&nbsp; trawler had similar results. They saw a 91% reduction in bycatch, and they still caught as much shrimp&nbsp; as they did without the lights. So it’s clear that light signals can have a&nbsp; powerful effect on specific species of fish, but it’s also not simple. When the researchers changed the position&nbsp; of the lights on the shrimp nets, it increased the bycatch. Which means we need to be careful,&nbsp; and learn about what kinds of lights affect what species and in what ways,&nbsp; in order to use them effectively. Now sustainable fishing is all about&nbsp; catching the right number of fish. Too many, and the population can decline. Overfishing can even lead to the&nbsp; sudden collapse of an entire ecosystem. But it isn’t easy to count every fish in the sea. We don’t even have basic data&nbsp; for over 80% of fish stocks. One idea is to bring in some help… in&nbsp; the form of artificial intelligence. Increasingly, fishing boats are required to have cameras onboard to film what they catch. This provides valuable data, but it takes a lot of person-power to review those tapes. So scientists have developed systems&nbsp; that use AI to automatically count, identify, and measure the&nbsp; fish that go past a camera. This tells them about how the ecosystem is doing, monitors bycatch, and helps&nbsp; enforce laws and regulations. Systems like these aren’t yet&nbsp; in widespread use anywhere, but they definitely have potential to&nbsp; help us understand what’s being caught. And AI could also help us count&nbsp; fish without catching them. For example, by analyzing footage from&nbsp; underwater drones or stationary cameras. This is particularly valuable as it can count young fish that are too small to be caught, so we can see how many fish will&nbsp; grow up to become adults soon. One of the most important aspects&nbsp; of managing a sustainable fishery is ensuring that enough young&nbsp; fish grow up to be adults, even if a lot of those adults are caught. A 2020 study attempted to use a&nbsp; deep learning program to count young mangrove snappers in&nbsp; over 12 hours of video footage, and achieved 95% of the&nbsp; accuracy of a human viewer! Of course fisheries management is only&nbsp; effective if the rules are actually followed. The ocean is a big place and in some cases,&nbsp; it’s all too easy to cheat the system. It’s estimated that illegal,&nbsp; unregulated, and unreported fishing makes up 30% of catches globally. This can undermine the&nbsp; sustainability of entire fisheries. A big part of the solution is to improve the&nbsp; traceability of the seafood supply chain. That lets you know the fish you&nbsp; purchase was caught legally, in a sustainable way, and it is actually&nbsp; the type of fish it’s being labeled as. And it turns out, that’s a big problem. A 2019 report found that 21%&nbsp; of fish in the United States were not actually the species&nbsp; they were being sold as. So one idea is to use blockchain:&nbsp; a decentralized electronic database most commonly known for powering cryptocurrencies. You might be tempted to roll your eyes at that, but there’s a surprising amount of serious&nbsp; consideration being given to this idea. The advantage is it isn’t controlled&nbsp; by a single organization or country, and it’s very difficult to hack since there&nbsp; isn’t a single point where records are kept. So the plan is to tag the fish as they’re&nbsp; caught with either an RFID tag or QR code that is scanned at every step in the&nbsp; process from the boat to your plate. It’s already been used in&nbsp; pilot projects to track tuna. And one day, this could even&nbsp; be directly connected to the AI systems we mentioned earlier,&nbsp; which would record the fish being caught to automate and verify the data being entered. But there are still lots of details to work out. In particular, blockchains tend to&nbsp; be notoriously energy-intensive. And a system that tracks sustainable fishing isn’t much good if it requires an&nbsp; unsustainable amount of electricity! So improving our world’s fisheries is a&nbsp; huge undertaking with no easy solution. There are a ton of promising&nbsp; high-tech ideas on the horizon, but we still have a long way to go. These technologies can be&nbsp; expensive, and implementing them requires governments and fishers to work together. But despite these challenges,&nbsp; sustainable fisheries are an important step towards&nbsp; improving the health of our oceans. Thanks for watching this episode of SciShow, and thanks as always to the coolest&nbsp; kids on the block: our patrons. Your generous support makes it possible for us to bring these videos to everyone, for free. If you’d like to get involved, you can&nbsp; get started at patreon.com/scishow. [♪ OUTRO]

  6. @Merennulli: We really need a large stick wrapped in a small amount of foam so that it's not lethal. We can then use that to smack anyone who refers to brokechain as if it were meaningful in any way towards solving a problem. (Using "brokechain" instead of the term that brokechain cultists prefer because 1. it's a solution looking for a problem rather than actually useful technology, and 2. bots key off that word.)

What brokechain does that they are wanting here is a procedurally generated non-sequential identifier and a database. Your fish is only going to be tagged with an ID, effectively just a large number, which would be generated by some procedure that ensures it's neither repeating nor sequential. Not being sequential is so that people can't just tag their fish with a number they expect the system to generate in the future.

Brokechain does this by working out difficult math problems and assigning the resulting large number to a fish. Unfortunately for this plan, there is nothing stopping an illegal fishery from reading a blockchain's data and randomly assigning existing ID to their fish, which is why decentralization is NOT what you want in an accountability database. An accountability database needs the data to be accessible only via comparison. In a database this is typically done by hashing a value, encrypting it in a way that is not reversible. Then you apply the same hash to the value you want to check and make sure the result matches what you stored. 

The only current way to break a hash is to have access to the hashing algorithm and generate a large number of comparison samples, which is infeasible for large values. This is why they aren't considered to be particularly vulnerable to quantum computing, nor to most realistic attacks. But critically, the other half of breaking a hash is that you actually have to have the stored hash. When you hear of a breach where people's data is stolen from a hashed database, that usually involves a very large database (called Rainbow Tables) of pre-hashed common text values to compare against the stolen hash. The longer the text strings, the more difficult this becomes (which is why you should be using 16+ characters, not 8).

Brokechain would record the value in a hash as well, but it's giving the hash and the hashing algorithm to everyone. That means nobody has to breach any system to start working against the hash. And importantly, they don't need a specific value out of the chain, they only need to break ANY number. With roughly 1 trillion individual fish being caught annually, they only need to guess and confirm via hash checking a few thousand per operation per year. While your fish brokechain is churning feverishly to calculate a specific value, they can spam numbers in the expected range far faster with far cheaper equipment until they stumble upon one that matches an existing hashed value.

And because you're relying on brokechain to ensure uniqueness, you have to do this as part of your node generation. For each of the 1 trillion fish. If you don't rely on it for uniqueness, people can generate their own illegal fish IDs in the system using a stolen hash value from a random fishing regulatory body and you'd just have to rely on "trust me, bro" to tell if their registry is real or not within the system. That means even with the smaller impact brokechain designs being used now, the processing power required is going to be insane for something that can be broken by brute force.

We have plenty of centralized systems that do this job FAR better in regulating things like banking transactions on global markets. Those operating illegally don't have access to the hashes outside of breaches, and when a breach occurs, the relevant data can be changed and rehashed to prevent ongoing exploitation.

And that's without getting into future proofing. Right now, brokechain is scrambling to adopt "post-quantum cryptography" options out of fear that growing available of quantum computers will leave them vulnerable. This isn't the first time cryptographic technology has had to scramble because smart people with improved technology found a way around it, and it won't be the last. Being public and decentralized sounds good in the democratizing feel of those words, but in practice it means that you can't hide things away while you're trying to fix them and you can't replace them without everyone seeing what you did and having an inroad into breaking your newest protection. You can make things secure with open technology, and open source does some wonderful things with this. But storing your DATA openly is not the same.

I described brokechain as a "solution looking for a problem" because fundamentally it does a thing that you don't actually want done when you actually think about it. It doesn't democratize things by decentralizing data processing and authority, it just mislabels "I did a thing with my expensive computer" as "authority". It doesn't protect data because the encryption is more vulnerable than proprietary data. And it doesn't ensure agreement over a transaction history, it merely mislabels "first" as if it means "true". All of the things it purports to do are no more true than a politician using stock phrases like "family values". Those of us who actually work in IT have been warning you for years that it's digital snake oil.

  14. @captainwull: The issue we have in the North Sea and North Atlantic is not declining populations of fish, its exactly the opposite. We have too much fish and not enough feed in the sea to support it. 
You only have to do a scale test on a Haddock to find that Haddock in the North Sea are seriously underweight. A four year old Haddock in Iceland weighs between 1.5 and 2 kilos. A four year old North Sea Haddock weigh between 400 and 500 grams.
The decommissioning of hundreds of UK and European Fishing vessels, along with drastic quota cuts in the last 20 years has led to fish populations spiraling out of control. 

The base chain of feed starts with Phytoplankton, a microscopic Plankton that depends on Sunlight, much in the same way as the leaves on trees depend on sunlight. They are the food that bigger Plankton eat, which small fish then feed on and then bigger fish feed on them etc etc. When you have too many of one species, which is the case with Haddock, the whole chain breaks down. 

Fishermen ARE the custodians of the sea, you guys need realize this.They need to be allowed to catch more in order for us to have a healthy stock. Fish are starving, eventually they will be come infertile and stop spawning. Then you will have a real problem on your hands.

You also showed Prawns in a trap. These traps can decimate the Prawn population. This is because Female Prawns very seldom come out of their Burrows, whereas male Prawns are out foraging most of their lives, so when a Prawn trawler fishes an area most of the prawns they catch are Males. Prawn Trawling is very inefficient, a typical Prawn Trawler will only Catch 20% of the Prawns they Trawl over. When we have done a survey of Prawn Creels the male to female ration is almost 50-50. 
Female prawns can store male sperm inside their shells for 3 reproduction cycles after mating, so catching Male Prawns is not an issue.

And by the way,  I worked in fisheries and marine science for almost 40 years. I actually do know what I'm talking about.

- 6 big ethical questions about the future of AI | Genevieve Bell: Kind: captions Language: en Let me tell you a story about artificial intelligence. There's a building in Sydney at 1 Bligh Street. It houses lots of government apartments and busy people. From the outside, it looks like something out of American science fiction: all gleaming glass and curved lines, and a piece of orange sculpture. On the inside, it has excellent coffee on the ground floor and my favorite lifts in Sydney. They're beautiful; they look almost alive. And it turns out I'm fascinated with lifts. For lots of reasons. But because lifts are one of the places you can see the future. In the 21st century, lifts are interesting because they're one of the first places that AI will touch you without you even knowing it happened. In many buildings all around the world, the lifts are running a set of algorithms. A form of protoartificial intelligence. That means before you even walk up to the lift to press the button, it's anticipated you being there. It's already rearranging all the carriages. Always going down, to save energy, and to know where the traffic is going to be. By the time you've actually pressed the button, you're already part of an entire system that's making sense of people and the environment and the building and the built world. I know when we talk about AI, we often talk about a world of robots. It's easy for our imaginations to be occupied with science fiction, well, over the last 100 years. I say AI and you think "The Terminator." Somewhere, for us, making the connection between AI and the built world, that's a harder story to tell. But the reality is AI is already everywhere around us. And in many places. It's in buildings and in systems. More than 200 years of industrialization suggest that AI will find its way to systems-level scale relatively easily. After all, one telling of that history suggests that all you have to do is find a technology, achieve scale and revolution will follow. The story of mechanization, automation and digitization all point to the role of technology and its importance. Those stories of technological transformation make scale seem, well, normal. Or expected. And stable. And sometimes even predictable. But it also puts the focus squarely on technology and technology change. But I believe that scaling a technology and building a system requires something more. We founded the 3Ai Institute at the Australian National University in September 2017. It has one deceptively simple mission: to establish a new branch of engineering to take AI safely, sustainably and responsibly to scale. But how do you build a new branch of engineering in the 21st century? Well, we're teaching it into existence through an experimental education program. We're researching it into existence with locations as diverse as Shakespeare's birthplace, the Great Barrier Reef, not to mention one of Australia's largest autonomous mines. And we're theorizing it into existence, paying attention to the complexities of cybernetic systems. We're working to build something new and something useful. Something to create the next generation of critical thinkers and critical doers. And we're doing all of that through a richer understanding of AI's many pasts and many stories. And by working collaboratively and collectively through teaching and research and engagement, and by focusing as much on the framing of the questions as the solving of the problems. We're not making a single AI, we're making the possibilities for many. And we're actively working to decolonize our imaginations and to build a curriculum and a pedagogy that leaves room for a range of different conversations and possibilities. We are making and remaking. And I know we're always a work in progress. But here's a little glimpse into how we're approaching that problem of scaling a future. We start by making sure we're grounded in our own history. In December of 2018, I took myself up to the town of Brewarrina on the New South Wales-Queensland border. This place was a meeting place for Aboriginal people, for different groups, to gather, have ceremonies, meet, to be together. There, on the Barwon River, there's a set of fish weirs that are one of the oldest and largest systems of Aboriginal fish traps in Australia. This system is comprised of 1.8 kilometers of stone walls shaped like a series of fishnets with the "Us" pointing down the river, allowing fish to be trapped at different heights of the water. They're also fish holding pens with different-height walls for storage, designed to change the way the water moves and to be able to store big fish and little fish and to keep those fish in cool, clear running water. This fish-trap system was a way to ensure that you could feed people as they gathered there in a place that was both a meeting of rivers and a meeting of cultures. It isn't about the rocks or even the traps per se. It is about the system that those traps created. One that involves technical knowledge, cultural knowledge and ecological knowledge. This system is old. Some archaeologists think it's as old as 40,000 years. The last time we have its recorded uses is in the nineteen teens. It's had remarkable longevity and incredible scale. And it's an inspiration to me. And a photo of the weir is on our walls here at the Institute, to remind us of the promise and the challenge of building something meaningful. And to remind us that we're building systems in a place where people have built systems and sustained those same systems for generations. It isn't just our history, it's our legacy as we seek to establish a new branch of engineering. To build on that legacy and our sense of purpose, I think we need a clear framework for asking questions about the future. Questions for which there aren't ready or easy answers. Here, the point is the asking of the questions. We believe you need to go beyond the traditional approach of problem-solving, to the more complicated one of question asking and question framing. Because in so doing, you open up all kinds of new possibilities and new challenges. For me, right now, there are six big questions that frame our approach for taking AI safely, sustainably Questions about autonomy, agency, assurance, indicators, interfaces and intentionality. The first question we ask is a simple one. Is the system autonomous? Think back to that lift on Bligh Street. The reality is, one day, that lift may be autonomous. Which is to say it will be able to act without being told to act. But it isn't fully autonomous, right? It can't leave that Bligh Street building and wonder down to Circular Quay for a beer. It goes up and down, that's all. But it does it by itself. It's autonomous in that sense. The second question we ask: does this system have agency? Does this system have controls and limits that live somewhere that prevent it from doing certain kinds of things under certain conditions. The reality with lifts, that's absolutely the case. Think of any lift you've been in. There's a red keyslot in the elevator carriage that an emergency services person can stick a key into and override the whole system. But what happens when that system is AI-driven? Where does the key live? Is it a physical key, is it a digital key? Who gets to use it? Is that the emergency services people? And how would you know if that was happening? How would all of that be manifested to you in the lift? The third question we ask is how do we think about assurance. How do we think about all of its pieces: safety, security, trust, risk, liability, manageability, explicability, ethics, public policy, law, regulation? And how would we tell you that the system was safe and functioning? The fourth question we ask is what would be our interfaces with these AI-driven systems. Will we talk to them? Will they talk to us, will they talk to each other? And what will it mean to have a series of technologies we've known, for some of us, all our lives, now suddenly behave in entirely different ways? Lifts, cars, the electrical grid, traffic lights, things in your home. The fifth question for these AI-driven systems: What will the indicators be to show that they're working well? Two hundred years of the industrial revolution tells us that the two most important ways to think about a good system are productivity and efficiency. In the 21st century, you might want to expand that just a little bit. Is the system sustainable, is it safe, is it responsible? Who gets to judge those things for us? Users of the systems would want to understand how these things are regulated, managed and built. And then there's the final, perhaps most critical question that you need to ask of these new AI systems. What's its intent? What's the system designed to do and who said that was a good idea? Or put another way, what is the world that this system is building, how is that world imagined, and what is its relationship to the world we live in today? Who gets to be part of that conversation? Who gets to articulate it? How does it get framed and imagined? There are no simple answers to these questions. Instead, they frame what's possible and what we need to imagine, design, build, regulate and even decommission. They point us in the right directions and help us on a path to establish But critical questions aren't enough. You also need a way of holding all those questions together. For us at the Institute, we're also really interested in how to think about AI as a system, and where and how to draw the boundaries of that system. And those feel like especially important things right now. Here, we're influenced by the work that was started way back in the 1940s. In 1944, along with anthropologists Gregory Bateson and Margaret Mead, mathematician Norbert Wiener convened a series of conversations that would become known as the Macy Conferences on Cybernetics. Ultimately, between 1946 and 1953, ten conferences were held under the banner of cybernetics. As defined by Norbert Wiener, cybernetics sought to "develop a language and techniques that will enable us to indeed attack the problem of control and communication in advanced computing technologies." Cybernetics argued persuasively that one had to think about the relationship between humans, computers and the broader ecological world. You had to think about them as a holistic system. Participants in the Macy Conferences were concerned with how the mind worked, with ideas about intelligence and learning, and about the role of technology in our future. Sadly, the conversations that started with the Macy Conference are often forgotten when the talk is about AI. But for me, there's something really important to reclaim here about the idea of a system that has to accommodate culture, technology and the environment. At the Institute, that sort of systems thinking is core to our work. Over the last three years, a whole collection of amazing people have joined me here on this crazy journey to do this work. Our staff includes anthropologists, systems and environmental engineers, and computer scientists as well as a nuclear physicist, an award-winning photo journalist, and at least one policy and standards expert. It's a heady mix. And the range of experience and expertise is powerful, as are the conflicts and the challenges. Being diverse requires a constant willingness to find ways to hold people in conversation. And to dwell just a little bit with the conflict. We also worked out early that the way to build a new way of doing things would require a commitment to bringing others along on that same journey with us. So we opened our doors to an education program very quickly, and we launched our first master's program in 2018. Since then, we've had two cohorts of master's students and one cohort of PhD students. Our students come from all over the world and all over life. Australia, New Zealand, Nigeria, Nepal, Mexico, India, the United States. And they range in age from 23 to 60. They variously had backgrounds in maths and music, policy and performance, systems and standards, architecture and arts. Before they joined us at the Institute, they ran companies, they worked for government, served in the army, taught high school, and managed arts organizations. They were adventurers and committed to each other, and to building something new. And really, what more could you ask for? Because although I've spent 20 years in Silicon Valley and I know the stories about the lone inventor and the hero's journey, I also know the reality. That it's never just a hero's journey. It's always a collection of people who have a shared sense of purpose who can change the world. So where do you start? Well, I think you start where you stand. And for me, that means I want to acknowledge the traditional owners of the land upon which I'm standing. The Ngunnawal and Ngambri people, this is their land, never ceded, always sacred. And I pay my respects to the elders, past and present, of this place. I also acknowledge that we're gathering today in many other places, and I pay my respects to the traditional owners and elders of all those places too. It means a lot to me to get to say those words and to dwell on what they mean and signal. And to remember that we live in a country that has been continuously occupied for at least 60,000 years. Aboriginal people built worlds here, they built social systems, they built technologies. They built ways to manage this place and to manage it remarkably over a protracted period of time. And every moment any one of us stands on a stage as Australians, here or abroad, we carry with us a privilege and a responsibility because of that history. And it's not just a history. It's also an incredibly rich set of resources, worldviews and knowledge. And it should run through all of our bones and it should be the story we always tell. Ultimately, it's about thinking differently, asking different kinds of questions, looking holistically at the world and the systems, and finding other people who want to be on that journey with you. Because for me, the only way to actually think about the future and scale is to always be doing it collectively. And because for me, the notion of humans in it together is one of the ways we get to think about things that are responsible, safe and ultimately, sustainable. Thank you.

- Mike Rowe explores how America's fishing industry provides food for nation: Kind: captions Language: en-US [MOUTH TRUMPET] ♪ but an offshore industry worth ten billion dollars a year. But it doesn’t come easy. Bringing the ocean’s bounty to your seafood section takes masters of strategy, gluttons for punishment, and a breed of worker bold enough to take on America’s last true wild frontier. How’s the old saying go? Give a man a fish and you’ll feed him for a day. But teach a man to fish and you feed him for a lifetime. Well, whoever came up with that, it apparently didn’t stick. Because it takes about 40,000 men and women to feed all of us Americans who never got our fishing lesson. Together, we consume about six billion pounds of seafood every year. Enough to fill more than 100 container ships. But getting that kind of bulk from the ocean to our table requires a fleet of commercial fishing boats operating day and night. And in the great Bering Sea, the world’s richest fishery, 7,000 of them, are trolling an area the size of Alaska. In search of the seafood needed to feed our nation. But none of them come better equipped for the job than America’s Finest. This 260-foot vessel represents the latest and greatest that commercial fishing technology has to offer. An onboard processing plant, freezers the size of football fields, and enough space inside to store three million pounds of freshly-caught fish. But despite all the bells and whistles, it still takes a crew of 50 to keep this floating fish factory in operation. All under the command of their intrepid leader, captain Darin Vanderpol. Our company’s given us the best tool in Alaska to do our job. Now it’s up to me and it’s up to my team and us to perform with it. We operate the boat 24 hours a day. We never stop, ’til we’re full. Naturally filling the hull with fish is always the goal. But there are other factors, like weather, food and fuel consumption that can force America’s Finest ashore before they’ve hit their quota. Something that happened their last time out. Leaving this fishing vessel in the red. We’ve got about a million dollars’ worth of overhead expenses. So we have to cover that every single time we go out. Last trip we came up short. We didn’t get the boat full. This trip we can’t do that. We absolutely have to fill up. If we do fill up that’s about one point seven million dollars right now of, product that we’ve put on the boat. That’s what we need to keep the company in the black. And that’s what we need for our guys to make money on the boat. On a typical trip, America’s Finest will stay out on the water for about two weeks at a time. And as it stands, they have just enough fuel for another day or two of fishing. Provided they don’t cover too much distance. In that time, they have to catch another 90 tons of fish in order for this trip to be considered a success. But so far their yield, from a once tried and true fishing ground, has been less than promising. Yesterday was not on par with what we need to do to, to reach our goal. We have to do better if we want to get the boat full. But we’re really targeting yellow fin. That’s what we want to see. This time of the year it’s almost like trying to find a unicorn. Captain Vanderpol’s quarry, yellowfin sole. They’re a species of bottom feeding flat fish similar to flounder or halibut. And to catch them he’s using a method known as trawling. We’ve got electronics like this, where we actually have a, a sonar that goes down with the net. So we get an idea of what’s going in the net in real time. After marking what should be their target species by way of sonar, a 300-foot net is deployed from the boat and allowed to sink to the sea floor. There, forward momentum forces a pair of underwater wings called doors apart. Opening the net to its full 900-foot width and swallowing everything in its path. So far this morning they’re two hours into their second tow and hoping for better results than they’ve been seeing lately. We’re gonna pull them up and we’re gonna see what we have for the catch. From there we decide what’s next. If fishing’s good, we stay. If it’s bad, we move. But in a place where relocating means covering hundreds of miles and spending thousands of dollars in fuel, suffice it to say it would be best to find the fish here. Let’s haul this back and let’s see what we’ve capture this tow and hopefully it’s a good one. But as the net nears the boat, Captain Vanderpol spots a more pressing issue. We got something going on with the door here. It’s not wanting to come up right. Taken off on its own here. Instead of coming up uniformly with the other one like it should be. I’m not quite sure what’s going on with that. Even on a boat as advanced as America’s Finest the trawling net can still take a beating from the Bering Sea. So all the captain and his crew can do is deal with problems like these as they arise. Hey Ale, you get me? Yep. Hey, it looks like we got something going on with the port door here. It’s acting squirrely coming up here. Coming in way faster. Might want to be back there when the doors come up, take a look and see if we got something going on there. While the deck crew investigates the squirrely door, deep in the bowels of America’s Finest the fish processing department is still busy cleaning, packing and freezing the boat’s previous catch. About 20 tons of yellowfin sole. And it’s up to guys like Luis Sanchez to ensure this highly-complex assembly line runs both quicky and smoothly. That is, when he’s not giving cameras the nickel tour. Once rinsed and weighed, the yellow fine are conveyed to a particularly sinister piece of machinery known as the header. After sorting the headless fish make their way to the packing area, where they’re placed into pans about 40 pounds at a time. Finally the pans of yellow fin sole are ready to be flash frozen, allowing America’s Finest to keep them fresh until it’s time to head ashore. It takes about two hours to freeze this 20 kilogram case of fish. Here we have our rock injector. What that does is separates the fish from this pan. It’s a stainless steel pan. It’s a system finetuned for maximum efficiency and minimal waste. But when working on a vessel as highly automated as America’s Finest, not all essential workers handle fish. Some, like chief engineer Steve Sauer, handle all the high-tech components that keep the vessel fishing 24-seven. But on a boat with this many moving parts, that’s a job that requires near-constant maintenance. I’m the guy that is responsible for all the systems on the vessel. This is one of the most modern boats out there. Very complicated, we have a lot of systems on this vessel. We are constantly doing engineer room rounds to monitor each and every system, and we got to check over every square inch of this vessel every day. Now we’re gonna go into the steering room. On a daily basis we’re just doing our normal engine room checks. We’re gonna go ahead and check sea oil level of our steering. Make sure everything’s good. Next, Steve checks the backup generators. Even though these generators aren’t running, we still want to make sure that they have an adequate amount of oil in them at all times. So that they are ready to run in the event of an emergency. We’re good to go there and we’ll continue with our engine room rounds. This is our bow thruster motor. It’s biggest, biggest actual electrical motor we have on the vessel, 650 KTM. Inside this tube right here is basically a propellor. And when we’re docking the ship, they have control to push the bow of the boat to the port or to the starboard. And this is how they steer the bow of the boat when they’re docking the ship. This is our hydraulic unit. Controls the pitch on the bow thruster. I’m just doing checks to make sure nothing’s leaking. Checking the integrity of all the hoses, all the hydraulic connections, just make sure everything’s tight. Hey, Steven, are you down there? Yeah, go ahead, what’s up? Yeah, we got an ICMS alarm for a bilge alarm in the engine room. Okay roger, I’m on my way.

Ice and snow is the only thing that will be used to make music. I find that hard to believe, but let's find out how they do it!" The stage was supposed to have been here - and it too was supposed to have been made entirely of ice. I inspect the rather sorry remains together with festival-employee Emile Holba. This is Europe's coolest - and perhaps most innaccessible music festival. The only way to reach Finse is by train. The Finse 1222 and it became a legendary place.” It was originally built to house the workers building the rail line. The ice planet supplies the raw materials for the Ice Music Festival. In December, festival organizers begin sawing blocks of ice out of the frozen Finsevatnet. I watch Even Rygg at work. Later, these will be carved into instruments. The organizers bring in a total nine tons of ice during the winter. The ice has a variety of qualities at different spots on the lake. There are several different types. It depends on how the water is floating when it's freezing. That's important for the sound. It's clear and not too many air bubbles, I think we can get some good sounds for the instruments. This is where all the magic happens! Terje Isungset cuts a piece for a marimba out of ice. [WHISTLING KEY] I want a higher pitch. Amazing Alright. Now, it's my turn! It's like butter! I'm really eager to try out this particular instrument: a kind of saxophone in ice, created by Norwegian jazz musician Kjetil Møster. It's hard to make and even harder to play. It's so hard!
