- Staying at a Traditional Japanese Inn | Ryokan & Onsen Etiquette: Kind: captions Language: en [Music] one of the best ways to experience traditional japanese lifestyle and hospitality is to stay at a ryokan or japanese inn popular with both japanese and foreign tourists these places not only offer a place to sleep but also a window to japanese culture as well as an opportunity to enjoy local cuisine and relaxing hot spring baths many different kinds of ryokan can be found around the country from family-run small establishments to large hotel-like facilities with hundreds of rooms budgets for a night stay also varies depending on the place you stay at ranging from 4000 yen without meals to over 50 000 yen per person per night with glorious dinner and breakfast a typical rate for a night's day is around 15 000 yen per person which includes dinner and breakfast because of the traditional style and atmosphere staying at a rokan can seem a bit intimidating for the first time guests however they're a special and very relaxing experience that everyone should take the opportunity to try at least once during their travels in order to set your mind at ease here is a guide on how to enjoy your stay at ryokan reservation dyokan can be found all across japan especially in hot spring resorts but also in the cities the typical ryokan includes two meals dinner and breakfast but some ryokan also offer no meal plans these days ryokan are listed in most hotel reservation websites and can easily be booked online note that most ryokan charged by person rather than by room and some dokkan do not accept single travelers keep also in mind that reservations for the same day are often not possible due to meal preparations that have to be made in advance before your arrival arrival staff aderogan will put great emphasis on your arrival and ensure that you will feel welcomed taken care of and relaxed the entryway of a ryokan called genkan is the physical boundary between the outside and inside world there outside shoes are to be removed and stored since they're not supposed to be worn inside once you've taken your shoes off it is considered good manners to turn them to point towards the door if a stone is present it is considered part of the outside and therefore you are supposed to step on it with your shoes on do not step on the elevated area with your outdoor footwear and likewise make sure not to stand in the lower area with your socks preferably wear socks instead of being barefoot and make sure your socks are clean and do not have any holes in some ryokan shoes do not need to be removed at the entrance but only in the room check-in occurs in one of three ways directly in your room at the lobby lounge area or at the reception desk just like in a regular hotel while filling the chicken form tea and snacks are usually offered in many dyokon you will be attended to by one person during your entire stay who will take care of your chicken serve your meals prepare your room and bid farewell when you leave we highly recommend checking in at least one to two hours before dinner which usually starts around six or seven pm to allow time to be properly greeted settle in and enjoy a nice relaxing hot bath before your meal your room yokan rooms can vary a lot depending on the size style and price of the dukan you're staying at they typically can accommodate two to four occupants have tatami floors with a low table at the center of the room and often have an adjacent smaller sitting room with western style table and chairs guests in adokan sleep japanese mattresses which are stored in a cabinet inside your room during the day and prepared by the staff in the evening during your meal typical amenities in ryokan rooms include a sink fridge toothbrushes a bathroom and western style toilet however in older establishments toilets and sinks are sometimes shared in a common area outside of your room it is important not to walk on tatami with shoes or slippers and to be careful not to dirty or damage it when carrying luggage slippers should be left neatly outside the tatami room when using the restrooms a different pair of slippers will be available for guests to use so they do not use the same ones used in the common areas guests will find bath towels in yukata which you can wear during your whole stay including meals to the bath and to bed here is a quick explanation on how to wear a yukata put on your yukata over your underwear and the shirts and socks optional fold the right hand side underneath the left hand side and hold it in place with your hand then fold the left hand side over the hand and hold it in place while you get your belt also called an obi wrap the ob around your waist cross it around your back and tie in the front for men it should rest fairly low on the hips for women the belt is tight at the waist finally adjust the knot so it lies on your right hip optionally in cooler weather the outer jacket can be worn over the yucata like a coat these coats may have a pocket for carrying small items such as your room key things to do staying at argan is synonymous with relaxing the inns often boast beautiful gardens baths and other common areas for guests to enjoy the most popular practice at erdogan is to indulge in a hot bath usually supplied by a hot spring although most ryokan have a whole range of gender segregated indoor and outdoor baths some offer mixed gender baths or private baths as well called kashkiriburo these private baths are made for families and couples who wish to bathe together and often require reservations before use at high-end places guests might also have a private hot spring bath in their guest room baths can be identified by the curtains that hang outside the changing room door blue is usually used for the men's bath in red for the women's bath mini ryokan switch the gender of the baths at a certain time of the day so that guests can enjoy all the baths offered by the facility here are a few etiquette rules on how to enjoy a bath at the bar you will need two towels a big one and a smaller one the small towel is used inside the bathing area the big one is left in the changing room and is used for drying your body completely baths are enjoyed naked so make sure to leave all your clothes and large towel in the changing room taking only your small body towel with you for washing drying and enhancing privacy wash or rinse yourself well with water before entering the bath and be careful not to put your body towel in the water finally dry yourself with your body towel before returning to the changing room so that the floor does not get wet and there you can use your large towel to dry your body completely another popular activity when staying at a dokkan is to enjoy the inns garden and grounds while wearing yukata in old fashioned hot spring resorts it is pretty common to stroll around the streets in yukata and enjoy some additional bath houses shopping or playing at old fashioned game arcades very large ryokan facilities often have karaoke rooms restaurants bars and game rooms which operate until late at night meals one of the highlights of arigan's day is getting to enjoy traditional and local japanese cuisine meals are often kaisekidori or multi-course japanese-style oat cuisine which starts with an appetizer followed by several dishes prepared using a variety of methods including raw dishes a soup a boiled dish a grilled dish a deep fried dish a steamed or dressed in vinegar dish and conclude with rice miso soup and pickles followed by a dessert dinner often starts at around 6 or 7 p.m and is served either in your room in a separate private dining room or in a communal dining area guests traditionally eat their meal while wearing their yukata although regular clothing is acceptable note that if you have any food restrictions it is important to notify the establishment prior to your arrival so modifications can be made to the menu when it comes to breakfast most ryokan serve japanese style breakfast consisting of rice miso soup and multiple dishes of local and seasonal specialties some will offer a western style option and some large ryokan may have a buffet-style breakfast which includes both japanese and western dishes just like with dinner it is totally acceptable to eat breakfast in your yukata or in regular clothing check out last but not least is the checkout which usually is at 10 or 11 am although many yoga now accept major credit cards smaller establishments may require you to pay the bill in cash once the bill is paid the staff including the person who attended to you during your stay will help you into your shoes and wave and bow goodbye as you leave the premises and there you have it all the nuts and bolts of how to stay in a yoga thanks for watching hopefully this video has given you the confidence to try out one of these traditional inns during your stay in japan for more information or to watch another video click the links on the screen now or head over to japanguy.com your comprehensive up-to-date travel guide - Japan's Most Popular Foods Explained: Kind: captions Language: en When you think of popular Japanese food, what do you think of… maybe sushi? Recently ramen’s been all the rage. Perhaps you’ve heard of karaage? Oh, but there’s so much more. We have yakitori, curry rice, yakiniku, sukiyaki, gyoza, tempura, soba, tonkatsu, hambagu, and I could go on and on, but we’ve got to stop somewhere. Not only am I going to introduce to you all these famous Japanese dishes, but I’ll show you the different ways they’re eaten, where they’re eaten, tell you a bit about their history, and hopefully you can learn a little bit about Japanese culture through it all. Hello World! This is Life Where I’m From, and let’s dig into some of Japan’s famous foods. Okay, so let’s start off with Sushi. Sushi is vinegared rice usually with some sugar and salt, plus a variety of ingredients called neta, such as vegetables, meat, or most commonly, seafood, which may be raw or cooked. In the early 1800’s in Edo, which is now called Tokyo, Nigiri zushi was born, which back then was essentially raw fish on top of vinegared rice. Back then, nigiri zushi was 2 or even 3 times the size of what it is today. So what in Japanese they would call Texas Size. I’m kidding, but I have seen Texas King Steak before! Now talking with my wife, she said that growing up in the 80’s, sushi was a special meal for her family. If I have to compare it to Canada, I’d say it sounds like it was similar to going out to a nice steakhouse for someone’s birthday or an anniversary. It wasn’t something you’d make yourself at home, and to this day, it still really isn’t. But it’s become ever more accessible. You can get it at a family restaurant, you pick it off a rotating plate, kaiten style, and you can grab a to go pack from a grocery or convenience store. Places will even deliver sushi platters to you. And there are so many kinds of sushi. Nigiri zushi is some main item on sushi rice that is shaped into bite-size pieces. Maki zushi is ingredients that are laid on a bed of sushi rice, which is on top of seaweed, a.k.a. nori, and is rolled up and then sliced into pieces. The seaweed is on the outside of the roll, not the inside. However, the famous inside out roll, or uramaki, is believed to have been invented by a Japanese chef in L.A. or Vancouver in the 70’s, depending on which story you listen to. That famous uramaki is the California roll, which surprisingly, I have found in Japan, but it’s a very rare occurrence. Inari zushi is a pouch of fried tofu that contains sushi rice. Chirashizushi is ingredients scattered over top of a bed of sushi rice. And then there’s temaki zushi, which literally means handrolled sushi. This is the kind of sushi that you will actually find being hand made in a Japanese home. We even featured this at a party at my sister-in-law Akko’s place. Sashimi, which is sliced pieces of seafood, is often confused as sushi, but in fact isn’t, even though most places that you can order sushi from would also serve sashimi. Sushi is often served with wasabi, pickled ginger, and soy sauce. And what most people think of as wasabi is in reality flavoured and dyed horseradish. Freshly grated real wasabi is expensive and rare… and also quite tasty on top of ice cream! And the best place to get sushi is at a specialty sushi shop, where you sit at the counter and they make sushi fresh for you right in front of your eyes. There are 3 rules that you might have heard about that I want to explain the nuances of. First, yes, you can pick up the sushi in your hand and eat it with one bite. Second, you’re not supposed to mix the wasabi and the soy sauce. The reason is that the wasabi will melt in the soy sauce diminishing it’s flavour. Plus there’s already wasabi the chef put in. If you want to add wasabi, just place it on top of the neta. Third, you’re not supposed to dip the rice in the soy sauce, as the rice can fall apart as well as absorb too much sauce ruining the delicate flavour. For using chopsticks, the chef taught Akko to tip the nigiri sideways first, then pick it up, making it easy to dip it in the soy sauce. I’m glad the chef let Akko do this part as he had me make sushi, and I was quite terrible. The last little thing I think is interesting is that it’s said you judge the quality of a sushi place by their tamago. If they can get that right, then you know everything else is good. Others will say that it’s the rice. Yakiniku is a relatively recent dish, with restaurants only opening up after world war II. In the postwar period beef and pork became a prohibited commodity and couldn’t be sold. But restaurants could offer the offal, so like the entrails and the internal organs, like the liver, heart, and kidney. And, when grilled up in Japan, these were called horumon yaki. Later on choice cuts like karubi and rosu, boneless short rib and lean roast, became the standard. Now when do you eat Yakiniku? I don’t think it’s considered an everyday item, but more something you’d make for social gatherings. It’s meat and other things grilled on a BBQ, so if we compare it to the U.S. or Canada, it’s similar to how people like to have a BBQ with other people while at an outdoor event, like camping or a picnic. A difference though, is that it’s also done indoors at home, and there’s even special electric grills that you can buy. I think the reason you wouldn’t do this inside your home more often, because it is much more affordable than restaurants, is that it’s messy. During cooking you want to open windows and run your exhaust fan. After you’re cleaning up all the grease from your table and floors. But it’s easy enough to do at home as they have the meats all sliced up and ready to go at the supermarket for you. Typically you don’t only grill meats, but veggies as well, like onions, peppers, potatoes, and cabbage, and any kind of seafood you might like. They’re often dipped in a sauce called yakiniku no tare, which is literally yakiniku sauce, which is a soy based sauce. My brother-in-law loves freshly grated garlic in his, which is tasty, but you don’t want to be around him afterwards. The easiest experience is at a specialty place, and while there are electric or gas options, sumiyaki, or charcoal is considered the best. The nice thing is that while you still need to cook the food yourself, you don’t have to clean up afterwards. Although you do pay much more for the experience, with similar cuts of meats costing 2 to 3 times as much vs. if you bought it at a butcher and made it at home. If you visit Japan and want to do your own yakiniku outdoors, many places will offer bbq rentals. Often you bring the meat, but rent the set, like what we did glamping in Kochi. Ramen is said to have come from Chinese wheat noodle dishes brought to port cities in Japan, like Fukuoka and Yokohama. It was in the years after World War II that ramen started to take root, as stalls called yatai would offer these cheap and nutritious dishes to customers. As ramen developed across the country, so did regional varieties, like tonkotsu ramen in Kyushu, shoyu or soy sauce ramen in Tokyo, shio or salt ramen in Tohoku, and miso ramen in Hokkaido. And there’s my favourite, tsukumen, which is noodles dipped into the soup bowl instead of being placed in it. My wife often likes mazesoba, which is a brothless ramen. It’s presented to you beautifully, only to have you mix everything up into a delicious mess. Of course there are more types of ramen, like tantanmen which originates from Chinese Sichuan cuisine and their dandan noodles. It’s one of those rare dishes in Japan that really is spicy. So depending on what part of Japan you’re visiting, you can experience a plethora of ramen varieties, which used to be universally under 1,000 yen, but with inflation, I’ve been seeing that number creep up past it at certain places. For Americans, at the current exchange rate, that’s like 7 bucks, taxes included and no tips wanted. It’s a very affordable working class dish that’s great for a quick get in and get out bite. The best place to eat ramen is undoubtedly at specialty shops that serve ramen and nothing else, where you’ll buy a ticket at a machine and then get served as soon as the bowl is ready. But you can get ramen anywhere and in any form nowadays. Family restaurant - check! Super sento - check! Instant - check Fresh cook at home noodles - check Take home collaboration packs from famous ramen chefs - check Frozen reheated noodles - check To go packs from your favourite specialty restaurant... Normally no, but during COVID there was a time where we were able to do this and we were actually touched by the taste, being able to again experience a flavour we hadn’t had for many months. In Japanese you would say 感動しました！ I was moved. While ramen has traditionally been a cheap, simple dish, chefs are upping the game with fancier ingredients and a ton of creativity. During the 70’s, 80’s, and into the 90’s there was a boom in Japan where it’s popularity soared. I don’t know about the rest of the world, but I’ve seen a similar boom in Vancouver, Canada over the past decade, where now it seems like you can find a ramen shop on every street. And while ramen is generally a quick eat and go noodle dish, it’s surprisingly not as fast as you would think. If you want fast, you have to go to the soba and udon shops, where you can be eating within a minute or two. This next dish is called hanbaagu, as in Hamburg, Germany, where the uber popular American Hamburger also got its name. The Japanese version is usually made from ground meat, like beef, pork, or both, mixed with onions, breadcrumbs, and egg. The most popular topping is probably a demi-glace sauce, but there are plenty of other toppings that can be put on, like daikon oroshi. Like many dishes featured, this became popular in the postwar period. Even though it was originally beef and whole cuts were expensive, ground meats like pork and chicken were not, and when fillers were added, like the onions, breadcrumbs, and eggs, it became an affordable dish you could make at home. In the 1960’s pre-cooked versions started to become popular, and they come frozen in a plastic bag that only needs to be boiled. This is a popular and profitable dish in family restaurants, and I must admit, it’s one of my go-to’s as it’s inexpensive and one of the items where you can get a calorie count that’s closer to 500 than 1000 if you don’t order it with the set that comes with rice and miso soup. There are specialty hanbaagu restaurants, or steak restaurants that offer hanbaagu, but my gut instinct is that only a very small portion of hanbaagu’s in Japan are made at these places. And I realize this is not a hanbaagu restaurant behind me. While a hanbaagu at a family restaurant will often contain a few potato wedges and perhaps some corn, green beans, and carrots, as mentioned previously, you can make it a set which adds rice and miso soup. It’ll usually come on a hot plate so that the sauce can splatter over you as you eat. This is a popular dish for bentos that you can pick up at convenience and grocery stores as well. Overall, it’s an inexpensive dish that because it’s bunless and rice is optional, feels healthier than its American counterpart. Tonkatsu is a breaded pork cutlet that’s deep fried into deliciousness. The katsu part of tonkatsu is said to come from the English word cutlet which comes from the French word “côtelette”. Cutlet. カツレツ. Yeah, katsu doesn’t sound that similar to me, but that’s most of katakana for me. The ton part means pig. So really, tonkatsu means pork cutlet. The origin story goes that it was first offered as a thin piece of breaded meat that was deep fried in a Ginza restaurant in 1899 and was actually called PORK CUTLET, or in Japanese, ポークカツレツ. But then the dish changed when, taking cues from how tempura is made, a thick piece of meat was coated in a thick batter and deep fried. It’s said that in 1929 a Western-style restaurant in Ueno called Ponchiken began selling the dish with the name “tonkatsu”. It’s typically served with shredded cabbage, rice, and miso soup and comes with a thick Worcestershire-style sauce aptly named tonkatsu sauce. I like freshly grinding sesame seeds into the sauce and adding a bit of karashi or spicy mustard. It can also be served with grated daikon. There are so many cuts nowadays, like menchi and mille feuille, but the standard cuts are roast and fillet. When you eat it at a specialty restaurant it’ll usually come with unlimited cabbage, which is said to help reduce the absorption of fat from the pork cutlet. And that cabbage is usually topped with ponzu or gomma dressing. gomma meaning sesame. To make tonkatsu on your own, you get a piece of pork, cover it in flour, then dip it in egg, then dip it in panko, and then fry it in oil. But since that can be messy, I think most people who eat it at home would take it out from a specialty restaurant or pick it up at a grocery store. Then at home, one would add side dishes to it. It’s a standard dish offered at family restaurants and is generally reasonable in price as pork is relatively cheap in Japan. While tonkatsu may not be the prime choice for a special occasion like an anniversary, there are special times that it’s eaten. In Japanese, katsu means win or victory, so before a big test or big game you might have a tonKATSU meal for good luck. Health wise it’s perhaps not the best idea, especially if paired with a Kitto Katto, a Kit Kat, which shares a similar superstition due to its name. Being a Buddhist country, eating meat in Japan was banned until just after the start of the Meiji restoration in 1872. However, wild birds such as pheasants, ducks, and small birds were not banned. These birds were called Yakitori, which literally means grilled birds. During the middle of the Meiji period, so the late 1800s, yakitori stalls opened up. And while chicken was expensive, they would skewer offcuts and offal, as well as pork and beef. While pork and beef are obviously not birds, the term yakitori, grilled bird, still applies to all of those skewered items as well. In the 1960’s broiler chickens, so the birds that are good for eating, not the device that cooks the birds, were introduced from the U.S. and yakitori really took off. Now as mentioned, anything can be placed on a skewer and be called yakitori, like chicken, pork and beef, but there are many versions that add non-meat items in between, like negi, asparagus, garlic, tomatoes, and cheese. The two main seasonings are salt or a soy based sauce called tare. I don’t think I’ve seen anyone grill yakitori in a Japanese home until we bought this little yakitori grill for a video about Japanese food technology for single people. But I have seen it brought home pre-cooked many times, either from the grocery store or from a yakitori stand or little restaurant. Like tonkatsu, they’re great to pick up on your way home to use as your main protein, and then add other side dishes to it. While there are specialized yakitori restaurants, which will be the best place to enjoy yakitori, the most common place you may dine in and eat yakitori is probably at an izakaya, a Japanese pub. Yakitori is generally not a main dish kind of item, although I’ve certainly used it as one. It’s more of a side dish or snack, and a great item to get at an izakaya, a food stall at a festival, or a yatai upon your exit from a train station. As far as pricing goes, they’re not going to break the bank, but there are usually cheaper ways to fill yourself up. Curry rice, or as the Japanese call it, カレーライス, came to Japan from the English, which came to them from their colonies in the Bengal region of India. Rice was used, not naan, because that’s the main staple in the region. So in Japan, if you go to an Indian or Nepalese restaurant, the default accompaniment to your curry is naan, whereas with Japanese カレーライス it’s rice of course. The taste is also completely different from Indian curry, at least to me, who thinks that West Indian curry is the best curry. Although personally it’s a toss up between dhalpuri roti and naan. And in case you couldn’t tell, my parents are from the West Indies, so that’s why the curry preferences. What I’m taking a long time to say is that Japanese curry is way too sweet and comes with rice, when instead it could be spicier and come with naan or roti. The standard Japanese curry rice comes with potatoes, onions, and carrots and can be used with pork, chicken, beef, or even seafood. But if you’re in the Kansai region, beef will be the default, whereas if you’re in Kantou, it’s pork. It was popular as military food, since it was well balanced with high nutritional value, and I’m suspecting also cheap and easy to make. It then became a staple in school lunches, probably also for the same reasons. My wife said that curry is a really great dish to make at home since you can put anything you want into the curry and easily put it together. People mainly only used to buy store bought packages for their roux, but nowadays people are getting fancier and using their own spices. So it’s a go-to dish at home, because as the military and schools found out, it’s cheap, easy, and nutritious. While you’ll find curry in so many places, from family restaurants to conbini’s, you won’t generally find them as high end dishes, unless you go to Kanaya hotel in Nikko and spend many yen for their beef curry, which was admittedly delicious. But, you will find fast food curry chains, like Coco Ichiban, where you can get your quick fix. And because I’m a bit of a hater of most Japanese curry, I have noticed that Japanese like to put curry in everything. Ramen curry. Tonkatsu curry. Curry udon. Curry pan. What else? Oh, there’s pouch curry you can buy that’s shelf stable, so you only need to boil it and serve. It’s great for when there’s an emergency like a natural disaster, but also in an emergency when you don’t know what to make for dinner. Tempura was introduced by the Portuguese, who also brought guns back in the 1500’s. If you’ve seen Shogun you know this. The origin of the word is from the Portuguese word “Tempora”, which I have no clue how to pronounce, which refers to the times of fasting. During fasting, the Jesuits were forbidden from eating meat and instead ate fish and veggies that were battered in flour and deep fried. Nowadays, tempura is made by coating seafood or vegetables with a flour, egg, and cold water mixture, and sometimes baking soda or baking powder is added to make the batter light. Then it’s deep fried in oil. Once cooked, it’s then dipped in tempura sauce, which is dashi, soy sauce, and mirin. You usually add grated daikon to the sauce, and at fancier places, they might offer up a selections of salts to dip your tempura into as well. While there are specialty tempura restaurants, it’s so much more common to find tempura at other places. Like if you eat at a soba or udon noodle shop, they often have tempura to go along with it. It’s probably more common to eat tempura outside of the home, or bring it home as takeout, then to make it at home. That being said, my mother-in-law will often make a batch of tempura every few weeks and then send them out to her family members. And now that I think of it, she also does this with gyoza, which I’ll get to, I promise. Like yakitori, tempura is not usually eaten on it’s own, but as an accompaniment to a dish. However, if you go to a specialty restaurant like this, it’s served one piece at a time like a fancy omakase sushi experience. If you’re a lover of tempura, this is the way. Soba, or more specifically buckwheat, has long been grown in Japan. It’s especially known for its ability to grow in harsh terrain and be harvested 2 or even 3 times a year. It wasn’t until around the Edo period in the 1600’s that they started cutting them into the buckwheat noodles that you now know today. While soba nowadays generally refers to buckwheat noodles, they can be mixed with wheat flour, so you might get a mix of 80% buckwheat, 20% wheat. If it’s pure buckwheat, they generally promote it as jyuwari soba, or 100% buckwheat. But if you go to Okinawa and have Okinawa soba, that’s 100% wheat flour. Or if you order maze soba at a ramen restaurant, this dish that originated in Nagoya will be made from wheat noodles as well. And if you order Chuka Soba, that’s the old name meaning Chinese Noodles, which are ramen noodles. A place I often visit are supa sentos, or super baths. Not only do they have bathing facilities, but they’ll have a restaurant, and they usually serve soba. It’s not uncommon to find a grinding wheel creating fresh buckwheat flour to make into noodles. The most basic version is cold soba that’s dipped in tsuyu, made of dashi, sweetened soy sauce, and mirin. It’ll have sliced negi and some wasabi, and that’s it. You’ll most likely be given sobayu, which is the water that’s left over after cooking the soba, and is chock full of minerals. You’re supposed to pour it into your leftover tsuyu and drink it. I personally find it too salty, so I’ll drink it straight up. Beyond zaru soba, you can dress it up as much as you like. Put it in soup, eat it with tempura, have it as a salad, or fry it up. Out of the dishes presented in this video, it’s considered one of the healthier ones. The 100% buckwheat version is gluten free and has a low GI relative to other typical carbs like udon, ramen or white rice. I mentioned eating soba at supa sentos, but a very popular way to consume it is at tachigui soba shop, which means stand up eating soba. They’re very popular around train stations, and are even sold in little shops on the platforms themselves. And by the way, even if the name has stand up soba in it, they may also have seats available. But the shop will be designed around finding whatever spot is available and quickly eating your food and going. To me it’s one of the healthiest fast food options in Japan, especially if you can manage to avoid the tempura and get some non-fried veggies with your dish. There are many specialty soba restaurants, which, I don’t want to confuse things, usually also offer udon noodles as well. Talking with a soba shop owner in Tokyo, he said that the numbers have dropped over the decades as other forms of foods have risen in popularity. When traveling around the inaka, the rural areas of Japan, I can’t really think of a region where tourists gather that don’t have homemade soba noodle shops. Soba is also quite popular as a to go item at convenience and grocery stores, especially the zaru soba type that’s easy to package and doesn’t need to be heated. If I had to equate soba to a food in Canada or the U.S., I want to say it’s as ubiquitous as finding a hamburger. They’re found in specialty shops, fast food shops, family restaurants, and almost anywhere that serves food. Soba is also easily cooked at home, and zaru soba is a popular summer dish. While the soup version is great in winter. And on the last day of the year, everyone eats toshi koshi soba in order to let go of the past hardships, since soba noodles are easily cut. Cut the noodles, cut the hardships. Soba is a very affordable and quick dish, which I’m sure has contributed to its long lived popularity. If you eat it at a fast food place, you can get a dish for only 500 yen. But if you got to a specialty restaurant that makes their own, you can expect to pay about double the price, which is still extremely affordable. Keeping the hamburger analogy alive, when I’m hungry, tired, and in a hurry, in Canada I often cave in and go to McDonald’s to get a quick fix. In Japan, it’s a fast food soba noodle shop, and my body likes me better afterwards as well. Oh karaage, one of my favourite forms of chicken. Akko likes it as well, since she made a whole video about it! However, I should clarify that karaage can be deep fried anything, but if someone in Japan says they want to eat karaage, it’s assumed they’re talking about the deep fried chicken version. As we learned with yakitori, chicken wasn’t really consumed in large quantities until broilers came over from the U.S. in the 1960’s, so it’s a relatively recent dish. Although, the act of frying and making karaage, it dates back to the 8th century from China, so it’s also a very old technique. So karaage is usually made with chicken thighs that are marinated with garlic, ginger, sake, and soy sauce. Then it’s covered in an egg and flour batter and deep fried. It can be a main dish that comes with salad, rice, and miso soup. But it can also be a side dish that goes with almost anything. It’s popular at izakayas, parties, bentos, and at home. You can also buy it at specialty shops, usually as a take out item. And to think of it, a good number of places that sell food also offer karaage. When I asked my wife what special occasion she associates with karaage, undokai, or sports day, was her answer. Parents will bring lunch to eat for the family picnic and it’s a staple item. Out of the deep fried items we’ve covered, like tonkatsu and tempura, karaage is the easiest one to make at home. Chicken thighs are easy to find anywhere and they’re even pre cut at the grocery store to the right shape. For cooking it’s also easy to throw it all together in a bowl and then do a few rounds of frying. The two main versions of karaage you’ll see are ones made with flour and the other with corn starch. But I’m told that every family has their own specific recipe, so no karaage is alike… unless you’re using the pre-packaged mixes. Since the eating of beef was basically banned until the start of the Meiji period in the 1860’s, sukiyaki couldn’t be eaten before then, however there is a report of a Shogun receiving marinated beef. Now, the start of the meat eating culture in Tokyo was with gyunabe, or beef hot pot, and that was basically considered a feast for the common people since they had previously been prohibited from the consumption of meat. Sukiyaki is a dish consisting of beef simmered in a soy sauce, sugar, and sake based broth with ingredients such as negi, shungiku, and grilled tofu. After cooking you dip it in raw egg. In the past, sukiyaki was usually only eaten at restaurants, like what we’re doing here in Kobe with Kobe Beef. This wasn’t a popular dish made at home, but a fancy treat. Think job promotion, anniversary, visitors from out of town. But nowadays, you can make it at home, and thin cuts of beef are sold at supermarkets and butcher stores, and wagyu producing regions also sell their own great-for-sukiyaki cuts. Although I should still say that the cuts of beef are top of the line and are not cheap. People at home generally go for a more humble experience eating nabe, which is the Japanese version of hot pot where you can throw any ingredients into a soup broth. While sukiyaki is an expensive dish, it’s also a quick to cook one, since you’re speedily cooking the thin slices of beef and then immediately eating them. Now this is only my opinion, but if I’m getting nice cuts of meat, I’d rather eat them yakiniku style since I don’t want to cover the flavour of expensive wagyu. Or have the chef cook bigger chunks of the meat in front of me, teppanyaki style. We’ve finally reached one of my favourite Japanese dishes, gyoza. Like with many Japanese dishes, it’s origins come from China. It’s said that Japanese soldiers initially ate them during the occupation of Manchuria and then after the war was over, they were nostalgic for the taste, and started to make and eat them in Japan. The definition of gyoza is a dough made from flour and cooked by wrapping ingredients in the dough, so it can be made in various shapes and with different ingredients inside. In Japan, the most popular form of gyoza is fried pork gyoza, or buta yaki gyoza. Inside it will be cabbage, nira, garlic, and ginger. And it can be dipped in a sauce that is soy based with vinegar and chili oil. Unless you’re in Utsunomiya, famous for gyoza, it’s not normally a main dish, but more of a side dish to accompany a bowl of ramen, or as a snack at an izakaya. You can pick them up at convenience and grocery stores, but you can also make them at home, although it’s not something you quickly whip together. My mother-in-law often batch makes them and will freeze them for future use. You can also easily buy frozen gyozas to quickly fry or boil up at home. Our family uses it as an emergency meal for when we don’t know what to do. While fried gyoza is the most popular, you can get it in soup as well. Some gyoza will be fried in a circle shape and is called wing gyoza. The flour crisps up to create a thin crunchy base. My sensei made this in our “What Japanese REALLY eat for dinner” video. Now as you may have noticed, all the dishes we covered are depicted on this tenugui, a traditional Japanese hand towel, that was handmade by Japanese craftspeople both in Tokyo and in Mie-ken. We previously made a whole video about the process, but I did want to highlight a few of the differences this time around. The inspiration was this osechi ryori tenugui. Beautiful, but there are so few shokunin that have the skill to do this, so we had to go with a different method of dyeing. This time we wanted to make it as colourful as possible, so we upped the colours to five, which also made the dyeing job more difficult. Like last time, we made a batch of 500 of these tenugui. We were also able to dye up another 200 of the previous design, so if you hadn’t been able to get your hands on one before, now’s your chance. Just check the link in the description. Or simply go to shop.lifewhereimfrom.com. Or just scan the QR code, we can do that now as well for all you people sitting on the couch in front of the TV. And oh yeah, there were two food items on the tenugui that we didn’t yet mention. Those, of course, were rice and miso soup. While they are usually not the main dish, they are staples and can almost go with any meal. If you have a meal and they say “do you want to make it a set”, the set almost always includes rice and miso soup. You can have them with breakfast, with lunch, or with dinner. Rice is additionally popular in onigiri form as it makes a convenient portable on-the-go meal that can be eaten anywhere. It’s equivalent to the sandwich in the United States and Canada. Rice can also be made into mochi, nihonshuu, amazake, crackers… into anything. It’s everywhere and the possibilities are endless. As for miso, the paste can be used as a dip for veggies, as a base for ramen soup, as a face mask for softer skin… Ah no, don’t do that. But like rice, you’ll find miso in so many things. As always, thanks for watching, see you next time, peace! What are popular foods like where you’re from?

- These 5 fishes Cause health problems and even death because of dangerous elements in their bodies: Kind: captions Language: en Among its inhabitants, fish hold a special place, providing sustenance and vital nutrients to countless creatures, including ourselves. Rich in protein, vitamins, and those essential omega-3 fatty acids, fish offer a wealth of health benefits, combating inflammation, safeguarding our hearts, and even warding off chronic ailments. Yet, this watery realm, while abundant, also harbors hidden dangers. Within the very creatures we seek for nourishment, certain species accumulate levels of harmful elements, a consequence of the delicate balance of the marine food web. It is these species, these denisins of the deep that require our attention for their consumption, especially in excess, can pose a risk to our well-being. The swordfish, a magnificent creature of the open ocean, admired for its speed and grace. Its namesake sword, a formidable weapon used to stun prey, makes it a prized catch for sport fishermen. But within this apex predator lies a hidden threat, mercury. As a large long-ived species, swordfish accumulate mercury in their bodies over time, absorbing it from the smaller fish they consume. This accumulation, known as biommagnification, means that larger, older swordfish harbor the highest levels of mercury, a potent neurotoxin. Mercury, also known as quicks, shimmers with a deceptive beauty in its liquid form. But this element with the symbol HD and atomic number 80 belies a sinister nature found naturally in the earth's crust. Mercury is released into the environment through various human activities including mining and the burning of fossil fuels. Once in the water, it transforms into methyl mercury, a highly toxic organic compound readily absorbed by marine life. For humans, exposure to high levels of mercury poses serious health risks. The nervous system responsible for our every thought, sensation, and movement is particularly vulnerable. Mercury poisoning can manifest in a range of neurological and behavioral disorders, from tremors and memory loss to vision and hearing impairment. The developing brains of fetuses and young children are especially susceptible to mercury's damaging effects. But the dangers don't stop there. Mercury also targets our kidneys, those tireless filters of our blood and our liver, the body's master detoxifier. Even our digestive and immune systems can suffer under mercury's assault. Pregnant women, young children, and nursing mothers are advised to limit their consumption of high mercury fish like swordfish to protect themselves and their vulnerable offspring. While the occasional indulgence in swordfish might not pose a significant threat for most adults, moderation is key. By understanding the risks and making informed choices, we can continue to enjoy the bounty of the sea while safeguarding our health and the health of future generations. Patrolling the ocean depths with an air of ancient wisdom. Sharks inspire both awe and fear. These apex predators, masters of their domain, have roamed the oceans for millions of years. Their sleek forms honed by evolution. Yet even these lords of the sea are not immune to the pervasive threat of pollution. Like swordfish, sharks, particularly larger species like the great white and mako accumulate mercury in their tissues, a consequence of their position at the top of the food chain. For centuries, shark meat and fins have been consumed in various cultures, considered delicacies by some. However, this culinary tradition comes with a hidden cost. The very biology that makes sharks such efficient hunters, their longevity, their position as apex predators, also makes them susceptible to accumulating high levels of mercury. Consuming shark meat, especially in large quantities or over extended periods, can lead to mercury poisoning in humans. The symptoms, as we've explored with swordfish, can be wide-ranging and debilitating, affecting the nervous system, kidneys, and other vital organs. The potential for harm is even greater for pregnant women and young children as mercury can interfere with fetal development and cause lasting neurological damage. Beyond the health risks, the consumption of shark meat and fins raises serious ethical and environmental concerns. Many shark populations are in decline worldwide, threatened by over fishing driven by the demand for their fins, often used in shark fin soup, a luxury dish in some cultures. Along the sundrrenched coasts of Florida and the American South, a silver flash heralds the arrival of the king mackerel, a prized gamefish known for its fighting spirit and flavorful flesh. Anglers revel in the challenge of reeling in this powerful predator. Its sleek body a testament to its speed and agility. But beneath its regal exterior, the king mackerel harbors a secret, a consequence of its position in the marine food web mercury. Like its larger cousins, the swordfish and shark, king mackerel accumulates mercury in its tissues throughout its life. As a predatory fish, it consumes smaller fish, each carrying its own trace amounts of mercury absorbed from their environment and diet. Over time, this bioaccumulation process concentrates mercury in the king mackerel's flesh, posing a potential risk to those who consume it. While the king mackerel's smaller size means it generally contains lower levels of mercury compared to larger predatory fish, it's still important to exercise caution. Frequent consumption, especially for pregnant women, young children, and nursing mothers, can lead to mercury building up in the body, potentially exceeding safe levels and causing harm. The distinctive, somewhat strong flavor of King Mackerel, often described as fishy, can be off-putting to some pallets. This pronounced flavor is another indication of its position in the food chain, as fish higher up tend to have a more intense taste. In the vibrant coral reefs of tropical waters, a curious creature captures the imagination, the puffer fish. With its ability to inflate its body into a comical yet intimidating ball when threatened, the puffer fish has evolved a unique defense mechanism. But this seemingly comical creature harbors a deadly secret within its unassuming form. Tetrodotoxin. Tetrodotoxin or TTX is a potent neurotoxin found in the liver, ovaries, and skin of the puffer fish. This deadly poison 100 times more toxic than cyanide. Disrupts nerve signals leading to paralysis, respiratory failure, and death. The puffer fish, through a quirk of evolution, possesses a resistance to this toxin, allowing it to safely harbor this deadly substance within its own body. In Japan, the puffer fish, known as fugu, is considered a delicacy, a testament to the country's culinary artistry and daring. Highly trained and licensed chefs meticulously prepare fugu, removing the toxic organs with precision, as even a minute amount of tetratoxin can be fatal. Consuming fugu is a risky indulgence, a dance with death that has claimed lives despite the rigorous training of chefs. The allure of fugu lies in its unique taste and the tingling numbness it induces on the lips and tongue. A result of the trace amounts of tetrodotoxin that remain even after careful preparation. This tingling sensation, a reminder of the fish's deadly potential, is part of the fugu experience, a brush with danger that some find exhilarating. However, the risks associated with consuming puffer fish far outweigh any culinary thrill. The potential for error in preparation, the lack of an antidote for tetrodotoxin poisoning, and the ethical considerations of consuming a creature that could easily end a human life make fugu a dish best admired from a distance. In the vast expanse of the open ocean, a silver torpedo slices through the water. The Bluefin tuna. Prized for its rich, buttery flesh, Bluefin tuna is a staple of sushi restaurants worldwide. But beneath its prized exterior, the Bluefin tuna faces a growing threat, mercury contamination. This bioaccumulation process concentrates mercury in the bluefin tuna's tissues, posing health risks to humans. Sustainable fishing practices and consumer awareness are crucial to ensuring the survival of these magnificent creatures. Thanks for watching. - 5 of The Healthiest Fish to Eat and 5 to Avoid: Kind: captions Language: en-US 5 Of The Healthiest Fish To Eat And 5 To Avoid No doubt, fish can be a healthy addition to your diet. It is delicious, provides you with many essential nutrients, and is a perfect source of omega-3 fatty acids. According to American dietary guidelines, we are supposed to be eating fish twice a week. But there is also a danger of high levels of mercury, PCBs, and environmental concern. So how to choose the best fish that is good for you and the environment? Don’t worry! We have got you covered. In today's video, we will share with you the 5 healthiest fish to eat and the 5 to avoid. 5 HEALTHIEST FISH TO EAT #1. Herring Herring has the highest omega-3 fatty acids content compared to most fish. The omega-3 fatty acid is essential to the human body for many cellular functions. However, our body cannot synthesize this nutrient independently, so it is needed from external sources. Another plus point of herring fish is that it has a lower mercury content. It contains other valuable nutrients like vitamin D and selenium. Nutrition experts recommend incorporating this fish into your regular diet to keep cardiovascular problems at bay. It is also an excellent superfood for pregnant women since it reinforces brain development. Befriend your local fishmonger to help figure out the best methods of sourcing your favorite fish. It has many small bones in them, they can be eaten and serve as a source of calcium, but if you don't like bones, you can cut the herring open and debone it before cooking it. You can use this fish in many courses or side snacks, and it will taste delicious! #2. Rainbow Trout (and some types of Lake) Rainbow trout is low in mercury and sustainable fish and is deemed the “best choice” by the Food and Drug Administration. It is a beautiful, colorful fish with rich content of omega-3 fatty acids. It is an excellent source of protein for those who do not eat poultry or meat. It is high in potassium which is sure to aid in managing blood pressure, kidney disorders, stress, and anxiety. Another important mineral present in rainbow trout is phosphorus. It helps maintain the integrity of bones and facilitates an effective digestive system. The B-complex vitamins in it play a significant role in cell metabolism. Selenium acts as an antioxidant, preventing damage from free radicals to the human body. Rainbow trout is delicious when sided with oven-roasted vegetables, grilled vegetables, potatoes, fresh green salad, and rice. #3. Salmon, Wild-Caught (including canned) This versatile fish is renowned for being one of the best sources of omega-3 fatty acids. Wild-caught salmon is preferred over farmed due to less exposure to contaminants and pollutants. Canned salmon is also an excellent option if you do not want to get it fresh. It makes a fantastic fit in any healthy, nutritious diet. It is good news for those on a low-carb diet since salmon contains no carbohydrates unless you add sauces or condiments. And, It still is a rich source of all essential minerals and vitamins that your body requires daily. A delicious way to consume salmon is by adding it to soup or stew or even whipping up a batch of salmon cakes using canned salmon. Either way, it gives you immense nutritional benefits. Remove the skin; to further decrease the contaminants and carcinogens. #4. Sardines, Wild-Caught (including canned) Did you know that sardines were named after 'Sardinia,' an Italian island? It is because of the abundance which was found there. They can be enjoyed fresh or canned, but many people use the canned version due to its high perishability. Sardines are particularly beneficial to pregnant women and older adults since they contain calcium and other vital nutrients. The vitamin D, B12, and omega-3 fatty acids present help prevent heart diseases, thanks to their anti-inflammatory characteristics. Sardines also contain protein and numerous minerals such as niacin, iron, potassium, magnesium, zinc, and phosphorus. This versatile food can be used in salads, crackers, or as the main course meal. However, individuals with uric acid or kidney issues should be wary of sardines and always discuss them with the doctor before eating the fish. #5. Atlantic Mackerel If you are a fan of mackerel fish, opt for the Atlantic mackerel instead of king mackerel due to its lower mercury content. These little fish are a significant source of nutrition. In addition to being high in protein and healthy fats, Atlantic mackerel also consists of vitamin B12, iron, potassium, and selenium. It also contains vital antioxidants that keep you energetic and prevent heart disease. The best part is that the gear used for catching Atlantic mackerel fish is super effective and unlikely to cause significant habitat destruction, making it an ocean-friendly option. According to the FDA, it is ok to eat Atlantic mackerel two or more times a week. It makes a very delectable meal when Atlantic mackerel is paired with steamed rice and tender steamed broccoli. The simplest way to cook mackerel is by just pan-frying the filets or barbequing. We've made it halfway through our list, and we sincerely hope you've enjoyed it so far. Please don't forget to subscribe to my channel. Hit the Like button and ring the notification bell if you want to see more content like this. Let’s move forward with the video! 5 FISH TO AVOID #6. Orange Roughy These fish are undoubtedly nutritious with low carbohydrate levels but do not be hasty in buying this fish because it has some drawbacks to it as well. Also known as slimeheads, these fish have a life of almost 150 years or more. It means they have been around long enough to consume plenty of unhealthy elements like mercury. High mercury levels can interfere with normal brain function and are particularly dangerous for lactating or expecting mums as well as infants. Another concern is that orange roughy is overfished, and most restaurants refuse to serve it. Apart from stock concerns, orange roughy is caught by bottom trawling. It ends up destroying sea floor species assemblages and fragile seamount habitats. #7. Halibut, Wild-Caught (Atlantic) Halibut do not contain many omega-3 fatty acids. Other concerns are that halibut have high mercury levels and purine content. Purine breaks down in the body to ultimately form uric acid, contributing to gout and kidney stones. Another unsafe compound that it may contain is toxic industrial chemicals, such as polychlorinated biphenyls. Due to its low population, it has been overfished and now earned its place on the "avoid" list. It is not expected to repopulate until the year 2056. Opt for the farm-raised halibut instead of the overfished Atlantic halibut to improve the environment. #8. Salmon, Farmed in Pens (Atlantic) Atlantic salmon contributes to pollution and interspecies mixing, polluting the surrounding environment with fish waste, excess fish feed, medicines given to fish, and cleaning chemicals used for cleaning fish pens. They are often treated with antibiotics to combat diseases and sea lice. Farmed salmon are given a processed, high protein and high-fat diet to make them unnaturally larger. They contain fewer nutrients compared to wild-caught salmon and have higher fat content. Fishery advisors continuously work on consumer pressure to encourage fish farms to adopt better farming practices. #9. Bluefin Tuna Bluefin tuna contains high mercury levels and PCBs, so the ECF discourages consuming this fish. The World Wildlife Fund (WWF) stated bluefin tuna is an endangered species and warned that the fish population is depleting as it is overfished. Instead, you can substitute it with tinned sardines or even organic tinned chicken. #10. King Mackerel Mackerel is a particularly brilliant source of omega-3s to be incorporated into your regular However, king mackerel, which is caught in the Pacific Ocean, is full of mercury. King mackerel is a vast predator fish, closest to the top of a food chain. Consuming excessive oily fish like king mackerel can impact the body's capacity to fight against bacterial infections. All fish advisories say that children, pregnant, breastfeeding women, and the elderly should avoid this fish. This list might change over time because the industry is changing. Unless there is proper management, there can be a severe depletion rebound in a few years. If ever in doubt, check Monterey Bay Aquarium's Seafood Watch or NOAA Fisheries for detailed updates on various fisheries tracked by the US government. Overall, fish is a highly healthy, nutritious food and delight to the taste buds, and you should definitely add it to your diet. Do you find this video informative or helpful? Let us know in the comment section below. Make sure to subscribe so you don’t miss out on my future videos. Don’t forget to like and share. Stay Healthy.

- 全球10大健康海鮮排行榜曝光！鮭魚竟只排第7，第1名便宜又好吃，90%的人從沒注意過！: Kind: captions Language: en Today we're going to talk about the world's top ten healthiest seafood. Some people might think that salmon is the first fish that comes to mind when it comes to nutritious fish. However, surprisingly, salmon only ranks seventh on this list of healthy seafood! So the question is, which seafood surpasses salmon and occupies the top six spots? And what exactly is the champion? It's said that the number one seafood is not only delicious and inexpensive, but also incredibly nutritious. Unfortunately, many people are unaware of its value . Let's delve into these ten healthy seafoods on the list and their respective nutritional secrets. Number ten is tuna. Tuna is arguably one of the most well-known fish globally, frequently seen in sashimi, teppanyaki, and canned goods. As a large, deep-sea fish, tuna has firm flesh, is rich in protein, and contains significant amounts of Omega-3 fatty acids, vitamin D, vitamin B12, selenium, and other nutrients. For example, many people enjoy canned tuna, which is not only convenient to preserve but also provides the body with DHA and EPA, beneficial for cardiovascular and brain health . However, tuna only ranks tenth on the list, mainly due to potential heavy metal concerns . Because of their large size and long lifespan, tuna occupies a high position in the food chain, making them prone to accumulating large amounts of heavy metals such as mercury. Long-term consumption of large tuna may lead to high mercury levels in the body, potentially damaging the nervous system . Therefore, experts recommend that adults eat tuna in moderation, while pregnant women and children should be more cautious. Fortunately, we usually... Most canned tuna consumed in large quantities uses smaller varieties, such as bonito, which have relatively lower mercury content. Moderate consumption is generally safe. Tuna is nutritious, delicious, and a good source of protein and Omega-3. However, due to heavy metal risks, it only ranks tenth on the health ranking list. Ninth is lobster. Yes, the expensive and luxurious lobster in many people's minds. You might be surprised that lobster appears on the list of healthy seafood, since we usually treat it as a treat. However, from a nutritional perspective, lobster performs remarkably well. First, lobster meat contains almost no fat but is rich in high-quality protein. Every 100 grams of cooked lobster meat contains more than one-fifth of the daily protein requirement , which is helpful for building muscle and maintaining physical strength . At the same time, lobster is also a treasure trove of various minerals, such as zinc, selenium, copper, and vitamin B12, which are present in considerable amounts. These nutrients help strengthen the immune system, participate in red blood cell production, and are also important for maintaining brain and nerve function. What many people don't know is that historically, lobster was once considered "food for the common people" during the American colonies. In the past, lobsters were even supplied in large quantities to feed prisoners. However, today, people have discovered that lobster meat is sweet and nutritious, and its value has risen to that of a high-end seafood . However, lobsters also have their drawbacks: they are expensive and not readily available. Furthermore, crustaceans are high in purines, making them unsuitable for people with high uric acid or gout. In addition, lobsters have a higher cholesterol content than most fish. Therefore, even though lobsters are nutritious and delicious, they only rank ninth in health rankings. They are good as an occasional health-boosting dish, but not suitable for daily consumption. Eighth place goes to crabs. Crabs and lobsters belong to the same crustacean family, and their nutritional value is no less than that of lobsters. Crabs are also more affordable and common than lobsters. Crab meat is sweet and delicate, high in protein and low in fat, making it an excellent source of protein. Besides protein, crabs are also rich in various B vitamins and minerals such as zinc, selenium, and copper. Their Omega-3 fatty acid content is outstanding among crustaceans, which is beneficial for cardiovascular health. While autumn's hairy crabs are delicious , we should also pay attention to our health while enjoying them: some parts of the crab, such as crab roe and crab paste, are high in cholesterol. Crabs are high in alcohol and fat, so moderation is advised , especially for people with high blood lipids. It's best to avoid licking the delicious crab roe while eating crab. In addition, crabs are high in purines, and excessive consumption may trigger gout . People with existing gout or high uric acid should limit their intake . However, generally healthy adults don't need to worry too much about these issues when eating crabs in moderation . On the contrary, the nutrients provided by crab meat are very beneficial to the body. Crab shells also contain a special component—chitin (chitosan) , which is said to have certain immune-regulating and blood lipid-lowering effects. Overall, crabs are reasonably priced, delicious, and nutritious , making them a worthwhile healthy seafood, ranking eighth on the list. Next up is seventh place—salmon. Salmon has become a household name in recent years. Many people think of it when they hear about Omega-3 fatty acids. Salmon meat is orange-red and rich in oil. The reason for its attractive orange-red color is that it contains a large amount of astaxanthin. Astaxanthin is a powerful antioxidant. Studies have shown that its antioxidant activity is hundreds of times higher than that of vitamin E and beta-carotene , helping the body resist free radical damage. This means that eating salmon not only provides EPA, but also... DHA and other Omega-3 fatty acids are beneficial to the heart and brain, and salmon also provides the anti-inflammatory, eye-protecting, and skin-care benefits of astaxanthin. In addition, salmon is also rich in vitamin D ; one serving of salmon can provide most of the daily requirement of vitamin D. This is especially important for people who spend a lot of time indoors and don't get enough sunlight , as vitamin D helps maintain immune function and bone health. Salmon is also rich in high-quality protein and various minerals, such as selenium, potassium, and B vitamins, making it a very comprehensive source of nutrition. Given that salmon has so many advantages, why is it only ranked seventh on the list? The reason isn't that salmon is bad, but rather that there are many superior competitors. First, salmon isn't the fish with the highest Omega-3 content. For example, mackerel of the same weight contains far more DHA/EPA than salmon, sometimes several times higher . Second, most commercially available salmon is farmed . While it's high in fat, the Omega-3 ratio may not be as high as wild-caught fish . Furthermore, farmed fish may have antibiotic residues or be affected by environmental pollution. In contrast, some smaller, wild-caught fish are often more natural and cleaner. Finally, salmon is relatively expensive and not a common dish that everyone can afford. Considering all these factors, while salmon remains an extremely healthy food , it only ranks seventh on this list, unable to achieve a higher ranking. Now I... Let's look at number six – shrimp. Whether it's sea shrimp or freshwater shrimp, shrimp is a popular seafood delicacy. Many people's first reaction to shrimp might not be "healthy" but "delicious." However, shrimp is not only delicious but also highly nutritious. First, shrimp meat is high in protein and low in fat and calories, making it an ideal source of high-quality protein. 100 grams of shrimp contains roughly the same amount of protein as a cup of milk , but its fat and calorie content is far lower than that of red-fleshed shrimp. The orange-red color of shrimp also comes from astaxanthin, which, like salmon, is rich in the same antioxidants. Astaxanthin can protect cells, reduce inflammation and oxidative stress, and has potential benefits in preventing cardiovascular disease. Studies have shown that astaxanthin's antioxidant capacity is extremely impressive, even surpassing that of vitamin E and... Shrimp contains hundreds of times more beta-carotene. Besides its antioxidant properties, shrimp is also rich in vitamin B12, vitamin E, selenium, zinc, and iodine. Vitamin B12 and zinc contribute to nerve and immune function, while selenium is an important antioxidant mineral. In coastal areas, eating shrimp is also a good way to supplement iodine— iodine is an essential element for maintaining thyroid function. Many people worry about the cholesterol content of shrimp. Indeed, shrimp has slightly higher cholesterol than fish. However, the medical community generally believes that dietary cholesterol has a limited impact on blood cholesterol for most people . Eating shrimp in moderation will not significantly raise blood lipids. Moreover, shrimp itself is low in fat and contains heart-healthy Omega-3 fatty acids and lecithin . To some extent, this can offset the adverse effects of cholesterol. Therefore, there's no need to exclude shrimp because of cholesterol . In summary, shrimp is inexpensive, nutritious, and readily available. Whether steamed, boiled, or stir-fried, it can be a healthy dish. Its inclusion in the top six proves that shrimp's nutritional value is no less than that of deep-sea fish. Fifth on the list are clams. Don't underestimate clams just because they are small; their nutritional value is considerable. As a common bivalve mollusc , clams are a star ingredient in many family soups and stir-fries. They are affordable, delicious, and hold an amazing nutritional profile. First, clams are rich in protein and extremely low in fat, making them a high-protein, low-calorie healthy food. What's even more remarkable is the exceptional quality of clams in terms of trace elements and vitamins. Most notably, they are incredibly high in vitamin B12 and iron. Every 100 grams of clam meat contains far more vitamin B12 than the daily requirement. Studies show that clams even rank among the top foods in terms of B12 content, which is very helpful in preventing pernicious anemia and maintaining a healthy nervous system. Clams are also excellent for iron supplementation; their rich iron content makes them an excellent natural blood-building food for those with iron-deficiency anemia. In addition, clams contain various minerals such as zinc, selenium, magnesium, and potassium. Zinc and selenium, in particular, are crucial for immune function. Farmed clams are considered a source of... Clams are environmentally friendly and sustainable seafood because they obtain nutrients through filter feeding, requiring no additional feed. The farming process has a minimal impact on the ecosystem. Therefore, clams are not only nutritious but also low in mercury, safe, and sustainable. The only thing to note is that clams and other shellfish are best cooked thoroughly before consumption to avoid discomfort caused by bacterial or parasitic contamination. Overall, clams, being both delicious and inexpensive, rightfully deserve their fifth-place ranking . Fourth place goes to oysters, also known as raw oysters. Oysters have long been hailed as "milk of the sea," highlighting their rich nutritional content . In fact, oysters have an astonishingly high nutrient density; they are practically a concentrated pill of various nutrients among seafood . First... Oysters are low in calories but rich in high-quality protein. A 100-gram serving of oyster meat provides nearly 20 grams of protein, which is helpful for building muscle and repairing tissues. Even more impressive is the exceptionally high content of trace elements in oysters. Zinc, selenium, copper , and iron are all present in extremely high concentrations . For example, eating 100 grams of oysters can provide more than 100% of your daily zinc and vitamin B12 needs , as well as several times the recommended daily intake of copper. It also provides more than half of your required selenium. Zinc is essential for maintaining immunity and wound healing and is often referred to as "men's element" because zinc is closely related to male reproductive health, while copper and iron are crucial for... Oysters are a rich source of iron, and eating more of them can help improve iron levels and alleviate anemia. No wonder some say that a small oyster is like a natural nutritional pill, gathering many essential elements for the human body. Moreover, oysters are rich in vitamin D and Omega-3 fatty acids, which are beneficial for bone health and cardiovascular health. Of course, many people think of the sweet and savory taste of raw oysters with lemon juice. However, it's important to note that eating raw oysters carries the risk of contamination by pathogens such as norovirus. People with weak immune systems or the elderly should cook them thoroughly for safety. Fortunately, oysters are extremely delicious whether eaten raw or cooked. Steaming, grilling, or making soup preserves the freshness of the ocean and ensures... Overall , oysters, with their abundant nutrient content and enticing flavor, firmly hold fourth place on this list . Now, we welcome herring. For some viewers, the name herring might be unfamiliar , but if you mention classic pickled or smoked herring from Nordic cuisine , or the herring in Japanese cuisine, you might recognize this fish. Herring is a small to medium-sized oily fish, closely related to sardines, and shares many nutritional similarities. First, herring is extremely high in Omega-3 fatty acids, comparable to sardines and mackerel, making it an excellent source of fat for protecting the heart and maintaining brain function. Many studies indicate that weekly... Eating fish rich in Omega-3 two to three times a year can help reduce the risk of cardiovascular disease , and oily small fish like herring are an ideal choice. Besides Omega-3 , herring is also rich in vitamin D and vitamin B12. Vitamin D is especially important in regions like Northern Europe where winters lack sunshine, and herring provides a large amount of vitamin D, helping to strengthen bones and boost immunity. In fact, pickled or smoked herring has long been an important source of vitamin D for the people of Northern Europe. In the past, during times of scarcity, herring played a role similar to a "sunshine vitamin." In addition, herring is also high in calcium and selenium. It's best to eat it with the bones and fins. When canned or pickled herring, you can eat the cartilage along with it , providing a good source of calcium, which is beneficial for bone health. It's worth noting that while Asians rarely include herring in their daily diet, Asian saury is nutritionally similar, rich in Omega-3 and various other nutrients, and much cheaper. In Europe and America, herring is often sold in canned, pickled, or smoked form, making it very affordable and widely available. Therefore, some call it a "nutritious fish for the poor." In short, herring, with its comprehensive nutrition, excellent oil composition, and affordable price, ranks among the top healthy seafoods, firmly holding third place. Second place goes to mackerel. If we're talking about… Among fish repeatedly recommended by nutritionists, mackerel is undoubtedly at the top of the list. A traditional edible fish beloved by people in Asia and Europe, mackerel is a medium-sized oily fish with delicate, flavorful flesh. From a nutritional perspective, mackerel is almost flawless. First, it is one of the top sources of Omega-3 fatty acids. Nutritionists point out that every 100 grams of mackerel contains thousands of milligrams of DHA and EPA— a figure far exceeding most fish and even several times higher than the famous salmon. In other words, eating the same weight of mackerel provides several times the nutritional value of fish oil from eating salmon. No wonder some jokingly call mackerel a true "brain-boosting fish." Omega-3 helps lower bad cholesterol, raise good cholesterol , fight inflammation, and promote brain health. Eating more mackerel is a beneficial investment for the cardiovascular health of middle-aged and elderly people. In addition to being rich in fish oil , mackerel also contains extremely high levels of vitamin B12 and vitamin D , as well as a large amount of selenium. Vitamin B12 is essential for maintaining the nervous system and hematopoietic function, and mackerel contains more than ten micrograms of B12 per 100 grams, easily meeting the daily requirement. Selenium is a powerful antioxidant mineral and is also crucial for thyroid health and the immune system. It is worth mentioning that mackerel are migratory fish and often forage for small fish and shrimp in groups. They are not naturally occurring fish. As apex predators , mackerel accumulates relatively few heavy metals, making it safer to eat . In contrast, some large predatory fish, such as swordfish and sharks, are no longer recommended for frequent consumption due to their high mercury content. Mackerel is a prime example of a fish that combines high nutrition with low risk. Even better, mackerel is inexpensive and versatile in cooking. Whether it's Japanese-style salt-grilled mackerel, Taiwanese miso mackerel soup , or the canned mackerel commonly found in Europe and America, it satisfies both the taste buds and provides abundant nutrition . Overall, the seemingly ordinary mackerel is a true powerhouse in the world of nutrition , rightfully earning second place in this ranking. Finally, we reveal the champion—the number one ranked sardine! Sardines may not be as famous as salmon or tuna , nor do they often appear on the menus of upscale restaurants , but they are considered a "treasure of the ocean" by nutrition experts. Sardines are small, with silvery-gray bodies, and you can often grab a handful. For a time, they were considered ordinary, inexpensive canned food. However, modern nutrition science has discovered that the nutritional value of these small fish is almost impeccable. First, sardines are rich in the repeatedly mentioned Omega-3 fatty acids DHA and EPA, which are extremely high in these nutrients , greatly benefiting cardiovascular health, reducing inflammation, and maintaining brain function. Secondly, because sardines are small, have a short lifespan, and are positioned low in the marine food chain , they accumulate very little heavy metals, making them much cleaner and safer than larger fish . In other words, sardines provide... Sardines offer nutrition comparable to deep-sea fish but with virtually none of their pollution problems, making them safe to eat regularly . Furthermore, sardines are often eaten whole, whether fresh sardines are pan-fried, stewed , or canned sardines are enjoyed directly from the can. We usually eat the meat, skin, and bones along with them. This is remarkable because the bones and spines are rich in calcium and phosphorus , while the skin contains collagen and the eyes contain taurine, among other nutrients. Therefore, eating sardines is equivalent to simultaneously supplementing your diet with high-quality protein, fish oil, calcium, and other nutrients – a win-win situation. Sardines also excel in vitamins and minerals. For example, they are rich in vitamin B12, vitamin E, and elements such as magnesium, iron, zinc, and potassium. Vitamin B12, in particular, helps with red blood cell formation and nerve health. Vitamin E is an important antioxidant, beneficial for skin and blood vessels. Sardines canned often retain the fish's internal organs , which are rich in vitamins A and D , providing a near-complete range of fat-soluble vitamins. Even more appealing is the extremely affordable price of sardines; compared to expensive salmon and tuna, a can of sardines might only cost a fraction of the price, yet its nutritional value is in no way inferior . No wonder nutritionists highly recommend sardines and suggest incorporating them into daily meals. Unfortunately, many people still hold stereotypes about sardines, such as "cheap canned food" and "strong fishy smell," thus missing out on this bounty from the ocean. However, with increasing health awareness, sardines are slowly regaining popularity and becoming a new favorite among those seeking nutrition . In short, sardines, with their comprehensive nutrition, high Omega-3 content, low pollution risk, and high cost-performance ratio, deserve the title of "world's healthiest seafood," ranking first on this list . We believe you now have a whole new understanding of healthy seafood. Searching for nutritional treasures like finding a needle in a haystack, we've discovered that often those unassuming little things contain immense energy— the top-ranked small fish and shellfish are far more nutritious than many expensive large seafoods, fully proving the saying "concentration is key." Of course, this doesn't mean salmon and tuna aren't worth eating; they remain excellent sources of protein and fish oil. However, if you want to get more Omega-3, vitamins, and minerals, pay more attention to these affordable seafoods on the list. By cleverly incorporating sardines, mackerel, clams, oysters, and more into your weekly menu, you'll build a strong marine defense for your body, protecting your heart, brain, immune system, and bone health. We hope today's sharing has given you new inspiration when choosing seafood. Next time you go to the market, don't forget to give these healthy seafoods a chance to try their deliciousness and nutrition. Your body will thank you for your wise choice!
