- The Logistics of Music Festivals: Kind: captions Language: en This is the Glastonbury Festival… or at least,&nbsp; it will be. Right now it’s a dairy farm,&nbsp;&nbsp; set in the Somerset countryside, home to about 400&nbsp; cows. Through fall, winter, and spring they graze&nbsp;&nbsp; the fields for grass and shrubs, in turn producing&nbsp; milk that’s used to make Worthy Farm Cheddar. But&nbsp;&nbsp; come late-April, it’s time for them to relocate&nbsp; to their summer residence at a nearby farm.&nbsp;&nbsp; That’s because these fields are about to be&nbsp; transformed into the largest greenfield music&nbsp;&nbsp; festival in the entire world—in a matter&nbsp; of weeks, 200,000 people will descend onto&nbsp;&nbsp; this farm in the middle of nowhere Britain.&nbsp; First, they block off access—there are a number&nbsp;&nbsp; of roads and footpaths running through it which&nbsp; of course need to become private as construction&nbsp;&nbsp; begins. Next, the perimeter fence goes up, along&nbsp; with the very first facility: a preliminary site&nbsp;&nbsp; office from which the construction processes will&nbsp; be led. Before construction begins, though, they&nbsp;&nbsp; need to address the roads. Most of them are made&nbsp; of dirt meaning in Britain’s rainy weather, with&nbsp;&nbsp; a large number of heavy vehicles, they’d quickly&nbsp; become impassable. That’s why they lay down these&nbsp;&nbsp; temporary metal road coverings rated for up to&nbsp; 40 tonnes. There’s also the matter of bridges.&nbsp;&nbsp; There are a number across the site and small&nbsp; farm bridges were never designed to accommodate&nbsp;&nbsp; a constant barrage of semi-trucks, so while many&nbsp; have been permanently upgraded through the years&nbsp;&nbsp; to handle the demand, others get temporarily&nbsp; reinforced just for the festival period.&nbsp;&nbsp; Around mid-May, the core stage of the build&nbsp; begins, so the site office gets supplemented&nbsp;&nbsp; with shower and toilet facilities so that staff&nbsp; can camp on-site. Across the following weeks,&nbsp;&nbsp; this staff headquarters will continue&nbsp; to grow—they’ll add a first aid center,&nbsp;&nbsp; a welfare center with mental health and conflict&nbsp; resolution support, a staff canteen with three&nbsp;&nbsp; hot meals a day, even a number of crew bars.&nbsp; The bulk of the build-work involves erecting&nbsp;&nbsp; around 90 major temporary structures,&nbsp; ranging from tents to stages. What this&nbsp;&nbsp; looks like from the perspective of Glastonbury&nbsp; Festival Events Limited—the actual organizing&nbsp;&nbsp; company—is facilitating the work of a huge number&nbsp; of subcontractors who do the operational work of&nbsp;&nbsp; erecting each facility. Their staff does&nbsp; little themselves beyond, well, organize.&nbsp;&nbsp; As this occurs, work starts to build the core&nbsp; infrastructure of the event. Sanitation is of&nbsp;&nbsp; paramount importance—200,000 people is a lot, and&nbsp; if there are not enough places for them to go,&nbsp;&nbsp; they’ll find their own places to do their&nbsp; business. Glastonbury’s sanitation solution&nbsp;&nbsp; would be described by some as innovative&nbsp; and by others as horrific. Basically,&nbsp;&nbsp; they dig giant pits in the ground. They then&nbsp; install what’s called a long-drop toilet—a&nbsp;&nbsp; row of toilet seats on top of holes that drop&nbsp; into the bigger hole. The “innovation” is that&nbsp;&nbsp; the large hole is big enough that it doesn’t have&nbsp; to be emptied during the festival. Worthy Farm’s&nbsp;&nbsp; roads are massively congested during the festival,&nbsp; so having to have trucks constantly make their&nbsp;&nbsp; way through to empty out Porta Potties would be&nbsp; quite detrimental to broader festival operations.&nbsp;&nbsp; Rather, they clean the pits out just once at the&nbsp; end of the festival, then refill the hole with&nbsp;&nbsp; dirt and allow residual waste to biodegrade. Of course it’s not all about outputs—there are&nbsp;&nbsp; also the inputs. The festival includes about 1,000&nbsp; different traders—food vendors, craft stalls,&nbsp;&nbsp; charity tents, and more. Now, of course, it would&nbsp; be chaos if each and every one of these vendors&nbsp;&nbsp; had to be entirely self-sufficient—each&nbsp; stall would operate its own generator,&nbsp;&nbsp; you’d have propane canisters strewn all over the&nbsp; place, and the single-use plastics consumption&nbsp;&nbsp; would fly firmly in the face of the festival’s&nbsp; eco-conscious mission. That’s why Glastonbury&nbsp;&nbsp; essentially operates its own utility system.&nbsp; While Worthy Farm looks like a farm and operates&nbsp;&nbsp; as a farm, the fact that the festival was founded&nbsp; by the farm’s owners—who still own it to this&nbsp;&nbsp; day—made it practical to build some permanent&nbsp; infrastructure. The most notable example of this&nbsp;&nbsp; is its water supply system. Underneath the fields&nbsp; is a network of pipes, similar to that underneath&nbsp;&nbsp; any town, distributing water to key facilities.&nbsp; This has existed for decades, but the trouble&nbsp;&nbsp; was that demand over festival weekend was so much&nbsp; more than traditionally needed in the area—about&nbsp;&nbsp; 3 million liters a day compared to typical demand&nbsp; of just a half million in the adjacent village.&nbsp;&nbsp; Naturally, that demand spike strained the local&nbsp; water mains and led to low pressure for nearby&nbsp;&nbsp; residents. They complained that they were unable&nbsp; to do their laundry or take a shower during the&nbsp;&nbsp; festival, which wasn’t just annoying—it was&nbsp; a risk to the festival itself. When community&nbsp;&nbsp; sentiment goes down, so too does the likelihood&nbsp; of getting an operating permit. As a fix,&nbsp;&nbsp; the festival experimented with trucking water&nbsp; in using tankers and filling massive bladders&nbsp;&nbsp; that would feed into the pipes, but while this&nbsp; alleviated the water pressure issues it still led&nbsp;&nbsp; to community impact due to the nonstop procession&nbsp; of trucks snaking through small village streets.&nbsp;&nbsp; So eventually they built two massive underground&nbsp; reservoirs. Today, in the weeks leading up to the&nbsp;&nbsp; festival, these are slowly filled at a rate that&nbsp; does not stress the local system, and they have&nbsp;&nbsp; enough capacity to cover festival weekend without&nbsp; creating exceptional demand on the local system.&nbsp; The other utilities are provided by third-party&nbsp; contractors—the festival requires that all traders&nbsp;&nbsp; buy propane from the company Festival Gas which&nbsp; operates regular deliveries throughout the site.&nbsp;&nbsp; Meanwhile, another company called Aggreko builds&nbsp; a temporary electric grid for the festival,&nbsp;&nbsp; using a combination of large generators and&nbsp; renewable sources, and traders contract directly&nbsp;&nbsp; with them for their power supply more or less&nbsp; how they would with a traditional grid operator.&nbsp;&nbsp; This is only the start of the rules&nbsp; imposed on vendors by the festival.&nbsp;&nbsp; Each one of them is an independent business, but&nbsp; they’re perceived by attendees as a component of&nbsp;&nbsp; the overall experience, and therefore Glastonbury&nbsp; is accountable for them and their actions. That’s&nbsp;&nbsp; why they all have their binder: a huge&nbsp; collection of paperwork on which their&nbsp;&nbsp; ability to keep operating hinges. While exact&nbsp; requirements will vary, in 2015, it looked&nbsp;&nbsp; like this—vehicle and staff passes, proof of&nbsp; insurance, health and safety documentation,&nbsp;&nbsp; even their very own A5 No Smoking sign.&nbsp; But the vendors, for their part, have had&nbsp;&nbsp; plenty of time to prepare this documentation&nbsp; along with their staffing plan, exact footprint&nbsp;&nbsp; and layout of their space, and all the other&nbsp; minutiae required as the application window&nbsp;&nbsp; opens some nine months prior, with selection being&nbsp; made three months prior to the festival. Selection&nbsp;&nbsp; takes such a long time because a festival is not&nbsp; just trying to fill the openings, they’re trying&nbsp;&nbsp; to fill the openings with vendors that match the&nbsp; festival’s particular values and particular needs.&nbsp;&nbsp; From the nonprofit and jewelry tents, to those&nbsp; making greasy late-night meals or morning-after&nbsp;&nbsp; breakfasts, vendors inform the experience of a&nbsp; festival just as much as the art and music, so&nbsp;&nbsp; the Glastonbury planners pour over each and every&nbsp; detailed application. And should a vendor get in,&nbsp;&nbsp; they’ll be paying for the privilege. While&nbsp; exact fees to trade at Glastonbury aren’t&nbsp;&nbsp; listed because each is case by case, sizable&nbsp; and well-positioned food vendors have reported&nbsp;&nbsp; prices in the 15-to-20,000-pound range for&nbsp; the busier spaces nearer to major stages,&nbsp;&nbsp; with rates dropping the farther one is&nbsp; positioned from the festival center.&nbsp;&nbsp; A telling sign that the hefty upfront fee&nbsp; for a five-day run is worthwhile, though,&nbsp;&nbsp; is a particular feature that appears in&nbsp; small print on the Glastonbury map—the&nbsp;&nbsp; wholesale market. Essentially, it’s a shop for&nbsp; the shops. Each morning of the festival there’s&nbsp;&nbsp; a procession toward this spot as wholesalers&nbsp; bring in staple ingredients, fresh foods,&nbsp;&nbsp; and serveware for the depleted vendors to&nbsp; purchase. Much of these interactions take&nbsp;&nbsp; place in the morning when the festival grounds&nbsp; are generally quieter and easier to navigate.&nbsp;&nbsp; For a small window of time, a slew of trucks&nbsp; and tractors are permitted to run deliveries&nbsp;&nbsp; around the site very slowly and carefully. But&nbsp; for most, supplies are purchased and then hauled&nbsp;&nbsp; by hand or wagon to get ready for the next rush.&nbsp; &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Staying in stock is critical for vendors and&nbsp;&nbsp; the festival alike, as a vendor is required to&nbsp; station their booth at their assigned spot for&nbsp;&nbsp; the entire run of the festival—as leaving early&nbsp; would mean a significant fine and lost chance&nbsp;&nbsp; to come back next year. In part, this is down&nbsp; to security, as the vendor areas, or markets,&nbsp;&nbsp; function effectively as islands sealed off from&nbsp; the general admission areas. Booths, therefore,&nbsp;&nbsp; are impermeable extensions of fences, each&nbsp; keeping the festival public outside the&nbsp;&nbsp; market zone where vendors have their vehicles and&nbsp; extra supplies, and in some cases, where there are&nbsp;&nbsp; vendor-only services and camping areas.&nbsp; Even separated from general admission,&nbsp;&nbsp; running a vendor at a festival is a draining&nbsp; affair. The lines at some hours will be unruly&nbsp;&nbsp; and never-ending, and at other times, nonexistent.&nbsp; The heat will be sweltering, and there will also&nbsp;&nbsp; be unrelenting rain. All the while there will&nbsp; be pressure—pressure to move enough product to&nbsp;&nbsp; justify the entry fee, pressure to make enough&nbsp; to keep a food truck up and running for the next&nbsp;&nbsp; few weeks until another festival, and pressure to&nbsp; adhere to food safety standards—the last of which,&nbsp;&nbsp; the festival is also keeping tabs on. To help wrangle Glastonbury’s vendors,&nbsp;&nbsp; the festival deploys managers with offices&nbsp; placed near groupings of markets to ensure&nbsp;&nbsp; policies are being respected by the vendors and&nbsp; guests and offering themselves as a resource&nbsp;&nbsp; should anything start going sideways between the&nbsp; two parties. These Market Managers report to the&nbsp;&nbsp; Partnership Director, who is responsible for&nbsp; dealing with all the external partnerships—from&nbsp;&nbsp; fencing contractors to food vendors—and&nbsp; operates from the Event Control Center,&nbsp;&nbsp; which in 2015 was located here at the Green Barn.&nbsp; Beyond market management, much of the overall&nbsp;&nbsp; security and safety structures that the festival&nbsp; relies on emanates from the Event Control Center.&nbsp;&nbsp; At its busiest, it will see some 500 employees&nbsp; and volunteers working in and around it, while&nbsp;&nbsp; representatives of each aspect of the festival&nbsp; will be based out of it for the festival's&nbsp;&nbsp; entirety—such as the Operations Director,&nbsp; Infrastructure Manager, Security Director,&nbsp;&nbsp; Site Manager, and Health and Safety Coordinator&nbsp; to name a few. Directly neighboring the control&nbsp;&nbsp; center is fire control and a makeshift police&nbsp; station, the former supplied by the regional&nbsp;&nbsp; Devon and Somerset Fire and Rescue Service.&nbsp; Connected by phone, radio, and CCTV, the Event&nbsp;&nbsp; Control Center keeps close tabs on everything&nbsp; from impending weather and conditions across the&nbsp;&nbsp; ground to the flow of guests and density of crowds&nbsp; all with the aim to monitor pending problems and&nbsp;&nbsp; proactively gameplan solutions. And to make sure&nbsp; nothing is missed, and response can be hastened&nbsp;&nbsp; when problems do arise, the directors and managers&nbsp; stationed at the control center work with official&nbsp;&nbsp; Area Organizers spread across all areas of the&nbsp; festival—from stages to campgrounds. It’s this&nbsp;&nbsp; group that will supervise the buildout of their&nbsp; region, carry out daily inspections, and manage&nbsp;&nbsp; adherence on everything from health and safety&nbsp; standards to the onsite traffic management plan.&nbsp;&nbsp; Of course, keeping a festival running smoothly&nbsp; and safely during it requires ample foresight&nbsp;&nbsp; before it. A campground, for instance,&nbsp; can be well monitored by area organizers,&nbsp;&nbsp; accounted for by police, and aided by a robust&nbsp; lost-and-found system, but crime and petty theft&nbsp;&nbsp; are things that a good festival addresses&nbsp; long before the festival. At Glastonbury,&nbsp;&nbsp; campgrounds are modestly lit by strung-up festoon&nbsp; lighting to minimize crime in the dark of night,&nbsp;&nbsp; while each camp will have lock-up areas for&nbsp; campers' valuables. In such a chaotic environment,&nbsp;&nbsp; the best way to solve problems is&nbsp; to limit them in the first place.&nbsp; &nbsp;The same goes with crowd dynamics—crowd control&nbsp; procedures only really work when officials have a&nbsp;&nbsp; good sense as to the exact number of participants&nbsp; they’re dealing with. As a generally free-spirited&nbsp;&nbsp; venture from the beginning, Glastonbury has always&nbsp; dealt with its fair share of gatecrashers. In its&nbsp;&nbsp; history, the festival has even welcomed them—a&nbsp; past that has created a culture of seemingly&nbsp;&nbsp; good-natured trespassing. But that changed with&nbsp; this fence. Running 5 miles or 8 kilometers&nbsp;&nbsp; around the festival’s perimeter, the Super&nbsp; Fortress Fence, as it’s called, has helped the&nbsp;&nbsp; festival ensure a right-sized crowd by minimizing&nbsp; intrusions with its smooth 12-foot tall face and&nbsp;&nbsp; 45-degree overhang at the top. Still, some will&nbsp; try it, so spread across the festival are guard&nbsp;&nbsp; towers and patrols on motorized vehicles to watch&nbsp; for those trying to sneak over, or under, the&nbsp;&nbsp; massive fence. Perhaps countering the festival’s&nbsp; traditionally welcoming ethos, the fence is a&nbsp;&nbsp; safety necessity because letting in thousands of&nbsp; unaccounted-for guests will strain resources and&nbsp;&nbsp; alter crowd dynamics to a potentially dangerous&nbsp; extent. And it’s also a licensing necessity,&nbsp;&nbsp; as this fence came into being in 2002 in response&nbsp; to a 15,000-pound fine for having gatecrashers&nbsp;&nbsp; take the crowd size well over the licensed&nbsp; capacity. Without a far more aggressive system&nbsp;&nbsp; of keeping people out, Glastonbury was facing&nbsp; the potential of losing its license entirely.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Now, organizing a festival like Glastonbury&nbsp; once is, in and of itself, a massive feat,&nbsp;&nbsp; but doing a festival like Glastonbury year&nbsp; in year out is yet more impressive because&nbsp;&nbsp; it not only has to function, it has to function&nbsp; well. What’s at risk is not just losing business,&nbsp;&nbsp; but losing the festival’s right to operate at&nbsp; all. There’s no inherent right for Glastonbury&nbsp;&nbsp; to operate. If things go wrong, it risks&nbsp; losing this: its operating permit. And&nbsp;&nbsp; making Glastonbury’s operations yet more&nbsp; challenging is the fact that it has to&nbsp;&nbsp; do all of this in a quiet, sparsely-populated&nbsp; rural area of famously bureaucratic Britain.&nbsp;&nbsp; An endless array of regulations are imposed&nbsp; on the festival by both the local and national&nbsp;&nbsp; government. Perhaps the biggest risk-area, in the&nbsp; government’s eyes, centers around alcohol. It’s&nbsp;&nbsp; of course in the festival’s financial interest&nbsp; to sell as much alcohol as possible, but too&nbsp;&nbsp; much drunken debauchery can and has turned music&nbsp; festivals into riots. Therefore, the UK government&nbsp;&nbsp; prohibits certain activities that it deems likely&nbsp; to promote excessive consumption. For example,&nbsp;&nbsp; the venue must monitor to assure patrons are&nbsp; not pouring alcohol into other people’s mouths,&nbsp;&nbsp; and the festival is prohibited from running&nbsp; drinking games that involve consuming a certain&nbsp;&nbsp; amount of alcohol in a certain amount of time.&nbsp; But the government isn’t the only entity the&nbsp;&nbsp; festival is answerable to. Just as significant a&nbsp; risk is that of upsetting their insurers. You see,&nbsp;&nbsp; festivals are incredibly risky businesses, but&nbsp; they’re also massive businesses. Glastonbury is&nbsp;&nbsp; estimated to earn upwards of $80 million a year&nbsp; in revenue, but that revenue all hinges on just&nbsp;&nbsp; five core days of operation. If weather washes it&nbsp; out or key artists fail to show or reality doesn’t&nbsp;&nbsp; match expectations, Glastonbury will likely have&nbsp; to refund ticket-sales—either due to government&nbsp;&nbsp; regulations or public pushback. Of course, almost&nbsp; all of the expenses—contractors, tent rentals,&nbsp;&nbsp; infrastructure, staff salaries—will have already&nbsp; been paid for. It’s not like a traditional&nbsp;&nbsp; concert-venue where a year-round schedule offsets&nbsp; the risk of individual shows—here, all their eggs&nbsp;&nbsp; are in one basket. Without insurance, one rainy&nbsp; weekend could bankrupt the business, and even&nbsp;&nbsp; without foul weather the festival might struggle&nbsp; to hire contractors and artists due to concern&nbsp;&nbsp; about nonpayment in the event of cancellation.&nbsp; So insurance is effectively mandatory. Almost any&nbsp;&nbsp; large festival will hold a massive policy covering&nbsp; both general liability and more unique risks like&nbsp;&nbsp; event cancellation and artist non-appearance.&nbsp; Very few insurers—reportedly about ten—will&nbsp;&nbsp; actually underwrite these policies given&nbsp; their massive scale, giving them quite a&nbsp;&nbsp; bit of leverage in setting terms. Every policy&nbsp; is unique, but much of the design and operations&nbsp;&nbsp; of festivals is therefore dictated by the&nbsp; insurer—for liquor liability coverage they&nbsp;&nbsp; might require that sales are limited to two&nbsp; at a time, for general liability coverage they&nbsp;&nbsp; might set security staffing minimums, for artist&nbsp; non-appearance coverage they might prohibit the&nbsp;&nbsp; festival from booking particularly troublesome&nbsp; artists, for event cancellation coverage they&nbsp;&nbsp; might require an on-site meteorologist.&nbsp; But while these insurance policies make&nbsp;&nbsp; event management a sustainable business,&nbsp; they are also now holding the industry back.&nbsp;&nbsp; Many of these policies did cover cancellation due&nbsp; to communicable diseases, meaning the insurers&nbsp;&nbsp; lost a colossal amount of money in 2020 due to the&nbsp; COVID pandemic. Five years on, it’s still almost&nbsp;&nbsp; impossible to get a policy covering pandemics, at&nbsp; least at a reasonable cost, but even the policies&nbsp;&nbsp; excluding it have inflated in cost. There have&nbsp; been a number of high-profile incidents that&nbsp;&nbsp; likely involved high-dollar payouts in recent&nbsp; years like the deadly Astroworld crowd crush.&nbsp;&nbsp; And then on top of that, there’s a global trend of&nbsp; increased extreme weather. Just as this is pushing&nbsp;&nbsp; up home insurance rates in fire-prone areas, it’s&nbsp; also raising the cost of festival insurance.&nbsp;&nbsp; Combined, there are instances like the Bonnaroo&nbsp; festival, in Tennessee, which cancelled in 2020&nbsp;&nbsp; due to COVID, 2021 due to flooding, and 2025 due&nbsp; to heavy rains. With three cancellations in six&nbsp;&nbsp; years, it’s no wonder insurance rates are going&nbsp; up. Bonnaroo is owned by Live Nation which, due to&nbsp;&nbsp; its scale, can likely negotiate lower rates as its&nbsp; risk is diversified over many different festivals,&nbsp;&nbsp; but the impacts are more pronounced for&nbsp; independent festivals like Glastonbury&nbsp;&nbsp; where the risk is more singular. And insurance&nbsp; issues are greatest for new festivals. Insurers&nbsp;&nbsp; charge higher rates when a festival is unproven,&nbsp; which makes it exceptionally hard to start a new,&nbsp;&nbsp; independent festival—especially when Fyre&nbsp; Fest so visibility demonstrated the risks.&nbsp;&nbsp; But of course the other risk to the festival’s&nbsp; longevity is that the neighbors just get sick of&nbsp;&nbsp; it. And it’s happened before. In 1992,&nbsp; Anne Goodes, a Worthy Farm neighbor,&nbsp;&nbsp; erected a 30-foot white cross overlooking&nbsp; the festival site from her property. As a&nbsp;&nbsp; devout Christian, Goodes was antagonistic towards&nbsp; what she considered blasphemy taking place during&nbsp;&nbsp; Glastonbury, between the free love and free drugs.&nbsp; And it wasn’t just her—as the festival grew, so&nbsp;&nbsp; too did the schism between it and the locals—so in&nbsp; response, founder Michael Eavis developed a series&nbsp;&nbsp; of solutions that today fall under the tightly&nbsp; named Event Management Plan—one that’s recorded,&nbsp;&nbsp; approved, and filed by Somerset Council. These&nbsp; plans are designed to essentially contain the&nbsp;&nbsp; festival—succesfully implemented, it should&nbsp; make it so the neighbors in Pilton barely&nbsp;&nbsp; perceive Glastonbury, even as their village&nbsp; of a thousand effectively grows to 200,000.&nbsp;&nbsp; Of course, not everything can be contained. A&nbsp; certain amount of litter, for example, is to&nbsp;&nbsp; be expected, and therefore the festival commits&nbsp; to cleaning up at key sites like the surrounding&nbsp;&nbsp; streets and local train station for three weeks&nbsp; before and after the event weekend. Light and&nbsp;&nbsp; noise pollution are also inevitable, so a 12:30&nbsp; p.m curfew is instituted on the main stages on&nbsp;&nbsp; Friday and Saturday, along with a midnight sound&nbsp; curfew on Sunday. After that point, smaller stages&nbsp;&nbsp; and venues are allowed to keep operating under&nbsp; strict maximum volume conditions. In fact, to&nbsp;&nbsp; assure compliance, crews from the council patrol&nbsp; locations near the festival with decibel meters&nbsp;&nbsp; until dawn. And the same goes for lighting, which&nbsp; is installed specifically to “minimize the risk of&nbsp;&nbsp; nuisance” onto neighbors. Even the generators&nbsp; for directional lights have decibel caps.&nbsp; Of course, the one thing that just simply has&nbsp; to go in and out are the people. Transporting&nbsp;&nbsp; 200,000 people to the middle of the countryside&nbsp; is, of course, a headache. Naturally, public&nbsp;&nbsp; transportation is most efficient, least intrusive&nbsp; to neighbors, and it’s the method of choice for at&nbsp;&nbsp; least a third of attendees. That includes nearly&nbsp; 30,000 who take the Great Western Railway, which&nbsp;&nbsp; adds more than a dozen extra trains from London&nbsp; Paddington to the Castle Cary stop per day to meet&nbsp;&nbsp; demand. That stop, however, is not at the farm and&nbsp; from Castle Cary Railway Station—a tiny two-track&nbsp;&nbsp; stop with little infrastructure—officials help to&nbsp; guide the thousands of people onto shuttle buses&nbsp;&nbsp; which take them to the farm. Riders can only bring&nbsp; what they can carry—there are no trolley carts&nbsp;&nbsp; for luggage and large items like instruments just&nbsp; aren’t allowed. Buses carry another 20,000 people,&nbsp;&nbsp; who arrive in a cascade of times over the start&nbsp; of the festival and are dropped off at a dedicated&nbsp;&nbsp; entrance, close to the action, for coaches.&nbsp; And then, of course, there is everyone who drives,&nbsp;&nbsp; with 28,000 public cars, 5,000 campervans, and&nbsp; another 12,500 vehicles for employees. Traffic&nbsp;&nbsp; management starts nearly 30 miles or 50 kilometers&nbsp; away from the farm. First, there are 35 miles or&nbsp;&nbsp; 56 kilometers of designated clearways, where&nbsp; cars are not allowed to stop or pull over. Then&nbsp;&nbsp; there are more than 350 miles or 560 kilometers of&nbsp; diversionary routes, forced one-ways, and dozens&nbsp;&nbsp; of closed exits all designed to keep traffic&nbsp; moving and reduce impact on local neighborhoods.&nbsp;&nbsp; Here, roads are closed along Lottisham Road,&nbsp; A37, and A361 to keep cars concentrated on&nbsp;&nbsp; thoroughfares instead of traversing through&nbsp; farm lands and disrupting rural life. &nbsp;&nbsp; Residents, themselves, are given vehicle passes&nbsp; to make sure they can still move around between&nbsp;&nbsp; villages unimpeded. In fact, they’re given a lot&nbsp; of things. Residents of Pilton, the community most&nbsp;&nbsp; impacted, receive complimentary tickets to the&nbsp; festival and the surrounding neighborhoods get&nbsp;&nbsp; a number of price-tiered tickets along with&nbsp; a designated place to park at the festival.&nbsp;&nbsp; Officials also set up a 24-hour hotline for noise&nbsp; and light complaints. And, in the fall, organizers&nbsp;&nbsp; throw a Pilton Party—essentially a mini music&nbsp; festival—as a thank you to the villagers, workers,&nbsp;&nbsp; and local people. These efforts to work with the&nbsp; community instead of against it have worked. For&nbsp;&nbsp; 55 years, the festival has returned, continually&nbsp; growing in both numbers, stature, and scope.&nbsp;&nbsp; Music festivals are incredibly complex exercises,&nbsp; and they’re incredibly risky businesses,&nbsp;&nbsp; but there’s a reason why organizers&nbsp; go through all the effort and risk.&nbsp;&nbsp; Done right, they’re also incredibly successful&nbsp; businesses. They’re massively popular among fans,&nbsp;&nbsp; and also among artists. In fact, they’re some of&nbsp; the most attractive gigs out there—for headliners,&nbsp;&nbsp; the pay is massive; for emerging artists, the&nbsp; crowds are massive. And ultimately this makes&nbsp;&nbsp; sense. For a festival to build a 200,000&nbsp; person city on some empty cow pasture,&nbsp;&nbsp; there must be a very good reason why.&nbsp; So let’s say that now that you’ve learned&nbsp;&nbsp; about the logistics of music festivals you want&nbsp; to learn about the logistics of ski resorts or&nbsp;&nbsp; search and rescue or commercial fishing or the&nbsp; Hajj or coal mining or arms manufacturing. Well&nbsp;&nbsp; boy do I have news for you. We made a whole&nbsp; series called the Logistics of X that dives&nbsp;&nbsp; into those exact subjects. That’s one of almost&nbsp; a dozen different Nebula Originals produced by&nbsp;&nbsp; the Wendover team—we’ve also made a documentary&nbsp; about what happens when you're a tiny Alaskan&nbsp;&nbsp; town almost entirely dependent on cruise ship&nbsp; tourism and then COVID stops that entire industry.&nbsp;&nbsp; We made a documentary about the political&nbsp; kerfuffles that led to most of the western&nbsp;&nbsp; half of the US relying on the Colorado River&nbsp; to deliver them more fresh water than the river&nbsp;&nbsp; actually does—the consequences are bad, by the&nbsp; way. We made a documentary about what happens when&nbsp;&nbsp; you’re a tiny British territory in the middle of&nbsp; the South Atlantic who was only ever connected to&nbsp;&nbsp; the world by a week-long ferry but now you open&nbsp; an airport in 2016 and your entire way of life&nbsp;&nbsp; changes. And we’ve made even more than just that,&nbsp; but it’s worth knowing that it’s not just us:&nbsp;&nbsp; there are hundreds of creators on Nebula.&nbsp; Everyone uploads their regular videos there&nbsp;&nbsp; ad and sponsorship free, and plenty go on to&nbsp; make other great Nebula Originals. I think&nbsp;&nbsp; Wendover viewers would particularly enjoy 17&nbsp; Pages, which is a documentary by Bobby Broccoli&nbsp;&nbsp; about one of the biggest scientific controversies&nbsp; of the 20th century. It’s really well-made and,&nbsp;&nbsp; frankly, mindblowing. If you value high-quality,&nbsp; thoughtful, independent entertainment, then Nebula&nbsp;&nbsp; is the place to be. And in my opinion, what really&nbsp; seals the deal is that it’s quite affordable—when&nbsp;&nbsp; you use our link, Nebula.tv/Wendover—you’ll get&nbsp; more than 40% off an annual plan which brings&nbsp;&nbsp; the cost down to just $36 a year. That’s not per&nbsp; month, that’s per year. So this is far less than&nbsp;&nbsp; really any other streaming service or YouTube&nbsp; Premium, and signing up even helps support the&nbsp;&nbsp; channel. - These People Should Avoid Eating Too Much Meat on Eid! | Stop Overeating Meat on Eid!: Kind: captions Language: en Assalamu Alaikum viewers. I am Doctor Saba. In today's video we will talk in detail about a very important and practical topic. Who should eat less meat on Eid? Viewers, Eid ul Azha is a great blessing. Meat is available in every house. People eat happily. There is a family gathering. Different dishes are prepared and the atmosphere is very good. But from the health point of view, every person's body is not the same. For some people meat is very beneficial and for some people the same meat can create problems. Therefore, today we will understand in simple and human tone who should do quantity control and why. First of all let us understand what meat does in the body. When we eat red meat, it contains protein, iron, fat and essential nutrients that provide energy to the body. Makes muscles strong and removes weakness. But on the other hand, if it is over-consumed, it puts a load on the digestion system. It can increase cholesterol, increase uric acid and create many health problems. Therefore, balance is very important. Now we come to the first category of heart patients. Nazreen, if someone has heart disease like coronary artery disease, high cholesterol or has had a heart attack before, then they should be very careful. Red meat contains saturated fats that can slowly accumulate in blood vessels and increase the risk of blockage. The best advice for such patients is to take grilled, boiled or steamed meat. Avoid fried and oily dishes and portion control is very important. Meaning, do not eat too much quantity at once. People with high blood pressure: If someone has hypertension, they should also be careful on Eid. Because often people eat more spicy, salty and oily food on Eid which causes blood pressure to spike. Dehydration is also an issue for BP patients because salty food retains water in the body which can increase the pressure. Therefore, such people should take simple cooked meat. Excessive spices should be avoided and more water should be drunk. Diabetes patient Nazreen Eid is a challenging time for people with diabetes. Even though meat does not directly increase sugar, heavy fatty meals can disturb insulin resistance and make sugar control difficult in the body. Apart from this, if sweet things and drinks are also taken along with it, then the risk increases further. Diabetic patients should always control portions. Late night heavy eating should be avoided and a balanced diet should be maintained. Kidney patients. Now let's talk about kidney patients. Kidneys filter waste from the body and urea is formed after protein breakdown. If a person eats too much meat, it puts extra load on the kidneys and urea creatinine levels may increase. Therefore, kidney patients should always take meat as per the doctor's advice and the quantity should be kept very limited. Obesity and overweight people: Nowadays many people are facing the problem of weight gain. This problem increases further on Eid because people eat multiple dishes and consume a lot of calories. Red meat is high in both fat and calories. If someone is already overweight, they should maintain strict portion control and engage in physical activity like walking. People with digestive system Some people already have digestion issues. Like gas, acidity, constipation or IBS. Heavy meat can create a lot of problems for such people. Because it takes longer to digest in the stomach and causes heaviness. Such people should take boiled meat. Spices should be kept to a minimum and a light walk should be taken after eating. Small Children and Elderly People Nazarene children and elderly people have a weak digestive system. Therefore, they should always be given soft cooked meat and the quantity should be kept limited. Too much spicy and oily food is not suitable for them. Now a very important point, the biggest mistake that people make on this is that they eat too much food at once. The result is that the stomach gets upset. Vomiting may occur, acidity may increase and the body may feel very heavy. Therefore, the viewers should always remember that Eid does not just mean food but balance and moderation. Now let us understand some more important points which many people ignore. Nazreen, a very common issue is increased acidity and gas after overeating. When we eat too much meat at once, the stomach takes time to digest it and acid production increases, which causes chest burning and discomfort. If this happens, immediately eat light food like curd, rice or soup and give rest to the body. Now let's talk about timing. That on Eid, people keep eating heavy food repeatedly throughout the day, which disturbs the body's natural digestion cycle. The best practice is to eat two to three balanced meals a day and take a light walk in between. Now another important point is hydration. Viewers, many people drink less water on Eid because they are busy eating spicy and oily food. But due to less water, digestion becomes even slower and the problem of constipation may increase. Therefore, proper water intake throughout the day is very important. Now let us talk about kids and family habits. Children should always be given small portions and kept away from fried and spicy meat. Soft cooked meat or light gravy is the best option for them. The same rule applies to elderly people as their digestive system is weak. Now a very important lifestyle tip: If you want to stay healthy after Eid, then do walk for at least 20 to 30 minutes daily. This simple habit improves digestion and controls weight gain. Now let us clear a medical myth also. Many people say that meat should not be eaten at night. But the truth is that the problem is not time. The problem is quantity and method. If you eat lightly cooked meat in limited quantities, it can be safe even at night. Now the final advice is a happy occasion. But it is not right to ignore health. If you eat smart, you will enjoy yourself and also stay safe. Moderation is the key. - How to properly cut Tuna for Sushi & Sashimi!!: Kind: captions Language: en Today, I'm going to show you the correct way&nbsp; to cut a block of Tuna for sashimi & Sushi. I’ll be explaining the parts I&nbsp; couldn’t cover in this previous video. If you look at the Tuna, You might have noticed&nbsp; white lines running through the Tuna block.&nbsp; These lines are sinews, we call Suji. They&nbsp; are tough and difficult to chew through. Have you ever eaten Tuna sashimi or&nbsp; sushi and found it difficult to chew,&nbsp;&nbsp; leaving something in your mouth? This usually happens when the&nbsp;&nbsp; cutting method is incorrect, and&nbsp; it's because of those tough sinews. So, how can I avoid this? The key is to cut the Tuna&nbsp; perpendicular to the sinews.&nbsp; Doing this shortens the length of the sinews&nbsp; in each bite, making it easier to chew. On the other hand, cutting parallel to the sinews is a bad idea&nbsp;&nbsp; because it makes the sinews longer in each slice, making them hard to chew. Now, let me show you how to properly cut the Tuna.&nbsp; You need to cut at a right&nbsp; angle to the sinews every time. Now let's cut it. When you try it yourself,&nbsp;&nbsp; you’ll notice the sound of the sinews&nbsp; snapping as you cut through them. This is OK!!&nbsp; I was able to cut the Tuna cleanly&nbsp; without causing it to break apart! For comparison, I’ll also cut it the wrong way. When viewed from above, it might&nbsp; seem fine, this side is also fine,&nbsp; but this side will be parallel to the sinews. I'll try cutting it at this angle!! Wow, it's so hard to cut. When you compare Tuna cut the correct&nbsp; way with Tuna cut incorrectly,&nbsp; you can notice that the sinews in the&nbsp; incorrectly cut piece are generally longer.&nbsp; This makes it difficult to chew through and&nbsp; leaves an unpleasant texture in your mouth I would like to show the same thing&nbsp; using a different block of Tuna. Just like earlier, I'll cut&nbsp; perpendicularly to all the sinews. Alright, this is the correct way to cut it. Now, let's go ahead and try cutting it the wrong&nbsp; way. This side and this side are fine, but here,&nbsp; it's parallel to the sinews. If I cut this at this angle... Take a look!&nbsp; Cutting parallel to the sinews causes&nbsp; the Tuna to break apart slightly. It’s easy to see the difference between&nbsp; the right and wrong cutting methods. I’ll go ahead and explain again using&nbsp; that same piece of Tuna from earlier.&nbsp; For those who are confused, here is&nbsp; the easiest way to tell them apart&nbsp; if you’re right-handed, you should cut the Tuna&nbsp; while the sinews are running in this direction.&nbsp; That way, you can always cut at&nbsp; the correct angle to the sinews Even if you cut the Tuna correctly,&nbsp; and there are still some sinews left.&nbsp; In that case, you can always make a small&nbsp;&nbsp; perpendicular cut to break&nbsp; up the remaining sinews.&nbsp; This is called a “kakushi&nbsp; bocho,”a hidden cut technique If you look at this photo I&nbsp; took at a sushi restaurant,&nbsp; you can see that it had been cut&nbsp; at right angles to the sinews. Whether or not to use this&nbsp; kakushi bocho depends on the chef. To recap, it’s crucial to cut perpendicularly&nbsp; to all the sinews—this is something to remember! Also if the sinew looks tough, you can use&nbsp; a hidden cut technique (Kakushi-bōchō). For example, the otoro (the fattiest&nbsp; part of Tuna) has a lot of sinews.&nbsp; Some high-end sushi restaurants soften the&nbsp; sinews by lightly cooking the Tuna before serving It’s interesting how much effort goes into&nbsp; crafting even a single piece of sushi!&nbsp; Give this method a try at&nbsp; home next time you cut Tuna,&nbsp; and you’ll notice a big&nbsp; difference in taste and texture!

- Japan's Geography explained in under 3 Minutes: Kind: captions Language: en Welcome, in this video we will explore the Geography of Japan in just under 3 minutes. Chapter 1: The Numbers The East Asian Island Nation lays off the coasts of China, Russia and the two Koreas and in total consists of 6852 islands. Of these, only 421 Islands are populated and the 5 largest ones, Hokkaido, Honshu, Kyushu, Shikoku and Okinawa, house 99,3% of the population. Its land area makes it the 61st largest country in the world, but despite the relatively small size, it has the 11th largest population in the world. Due to Japans spread out islands, and the rule that a country is entitled to an exclusive economic zone or EEZ in short, for the first 200 nautical miles off of their coast, Japan has control of over 4.470.000 km2 of exclusive marine space for fishing and the extraction of mineral resources. This makes it the Nation with the 8th largest EEZ in the world, it is actually 11 times larger than the land area of Japan. Chapter 2: Physical geography Japans topography is shaped by the pacific rim of fire, like no other nation. 73% of the countries area is covered by almost 300 mountains with a height of over 1.000m and according to the Mountaineering book “Complete List of Japanese Mountains” there are over 18.000 named mountains and volcanoes, of which 108 are classified as active. These Volcanoes, combined with a very active tectonic boundary sitting underneath Japan, which regularly gives off earthquakes and tsunamis, gives Japan the 17th place in the ranking of the country’s most likely to be hit by a natural disaster. As a mirroring feature to the mountainous land, the sea in front of Japan is characterized by extremely deep ocean floors. In fact, 50% of the Exclusive economic zone has a depth of more than 4.000 meters, which makes it very hard to explore and nearly impossible to extract natural resources from. Chapter 3: The Peculiarities Probably Japans most outstanding geographical feature is the picturesque and highly symmetrical peak of the active stratovolcano Mount Fuji. This giant is not just the prettiest but also the highest mountain of Japan. On clear days, the peak of Mount Fuji is even visible from the roughly 100km away Tokyo. The Tokyo Metropolis is by far the most populated city of Japan, and when taking the entire metropolitan area into consideration, the Greater Tokyo Area houses 37,3 million people, making it the most populated metropolitan area in the world. And finally, the Shinano River in the center of Honshu, is the longest river of Japan with a length of only 367km, making it one of the shortest longest rivers of all countries in the world, excluding micronations and desert countries like Libya and Oman, which do not have any rivers. leave a like if you enjoyed this video and subscribe for more, cheers.

- Japanese Food Recipe - SIMMERED MACKEREL -  Professional Japanese cook technique: Kind: captions Language: en-US 20g of sake for 1L of water. Reduce the heat before boiling, then add sake. Blanch the fish.Lightly soak in hot water without completely cooking. About 5 seconds. [Before seasoning meat or fish, simply pass it through boiling water or pour boiling water over it to remove the odor of the ingredients. It also has the effect of making it difficult for you to get rid of umami.] Wash the fish with hot water. Remove the dirt that has come out by blanching. Removing dirt will improve the appearance and taste of the finished product. Make soup stock. 140ml water 60g sake 20g sugar 20g sweet sake 5g ginger Put the fish in the soup stock. Add 10g of soy sauce twice at different timings. Boil for 1 minute from there. Remove the scum. Turn off the heat and sprinkle the soup stock on the fish in the meantime to add color. It also improves the balance of taste. To dish up food, and complete.

- How the food you eat affects your brain - Mia Nacamulli: Kind: captions Language: en Your Brain on Food If you sucked all of the moisture out of your brain and broke it down to its constituent nutritional content, what would it look like? Most of the weight of your dehydrated brain would come from fats, also known as lipids. In the remaining brain matter, you would find proteins and amino acids, traces of micronutrients, and glucose. The brain is, of course, more than just the sum of its nutritional parts, but each component does have a distinct impact on functioning, development, mood, and energy. So that post-lunch apathy, or late-night alertness you might be feeling, well, that could simply be the effects of food on your brain. Of the fats in your brain, the superstars are omegas 3 and 6. These essential fatty acids, which have been linked to preventing degenerative brain conditions, must come from our diets. So eating omega-rich foods, like nuts, seeds, and fatty fish, is crucial to the creation and maintenance of cell membranes. And while omegas are good fats for your brain, long-term consumption of other fats, like trans and saturated fats, may compromise brain health. Meanwhile, proteins and amino acids, the building block nutrients of growth and development, manipulate how we feel and behave. Amino acids contain the precursors to neurotransmitters, the chemical messengers that carry signals between neurons, affecting things like mood, sleep, attentiveness, and weight. They're one of the reasons we might feel calm after eating a large plate of pasta, or more alert after a protein-rich meal. The complex combinations of compounds in food can stimulate brain cells to release mood-altering norepinephrine, dopamine, and serotonin. But getting to your brain cells is tricky, and amino acids have to compete for limited access. A diet with a range of foods helps maintain a balanced combination of brain messengers, and keeps your mood from getting skewed in one direction or the other. Like the other organs in our bodies, our brains also benefit from a steady supply of micronutrients. Antioxidants in fruits and vegetables strengthen the brain to fight off free radicals that destroy brain cells, enabling your brain to work well for a longer period of time. And without powerful micronutrients, like the vitamins B6, B12, and folic acid, our brains would be susceptible to brain disease and mental decline. Trace amounts of the minerals iron, copper, zinc, and sodium are also fundamental to brain health and early cognitive development. In order for the brain to efficiently transform and synthesize these valuable nutrients, it needs fuel, and lots of it. While the human brain only makes up about 2% of our body weight, it uses up to 20% of our energy resources. Most of this energy comes from carbohydrates that our body digests into glucose, or blood sugar. The frontal lobes are so sensitive to drops in glucose, in fact, that a change in mental function is one of the primary signals of nutrient deficiency. Assuming that we are getting glucose regularly, how does the specific type of carbohydrates we eat affect our brains? Carbs come in three forms: starch, sugar, and fiber. While on most nutrition labels, they are all lumped into one total carb count, the ratio of the sugar and fiber subgroups to the whole amount affect how the body and brain respond. A high glycemic food, like white bread, causes a rapid release of glucose into the blood, and then comes the dip. Blood sugar shoots down, and with it, our attention span and mood. On the other hand, oats, grains, and legumes have slower glucose release, enabling a steadier level of attentiveness. For sustained brain power, opting for a varied diet of nutrient-rich foods is critical. When it comes to what you bite, chew, and swallow, your choices have a direct and long-lasting effect on the most powerful organ in your body.

- 10 Rules For Reading a Food Label: Kind: captions Language: en Ten Rules for Reading a Food Label. It’s easier than gassing up a spaceship! Reading a food label, with all those scary words and numbers, is not as alien as you might think. We’ve created some straight forward rules that every earthling can follow. Rule 1: Food is fuel. The body burns food to live. Food is fuel for the body, the same way as spaceship burns gas. As it zips in between the planets, the body burns food as you go about your busy day. I know it sounds silly, but to understand food labels better, we will follow our alien friend as he gasses up his spaceship. Rule 2: Fuel is burned. This creates energy or calories. The amounts of energy obtained from digesting a set amount of food is called the Calorie. The longer the trip through space, the more fuel or energy the spaceship needs. In a similar way, the more active you are, the more calories you burn. Rule 3: Know how much is in one serving. Sorry, it’s not a gallon. Gas is measured by the gallon but food is a little more complicated than fuel. It comes in different shapes, sizes, and containers. To understand how much energy the food you are eating contains, you must first know how big the serving size is. Thankfully, this is one of the first things the food label will list. Serving size is typically provided in cups or ounces. So measuring the food you plan to eat is the only way to know exactly how many calories it contains. Rule 4: The calories listed are per serving. Please do not eat the entire box. The calorie listing is important because it tells you how much energy you get from eating one serving of food. Think of calories per serving the same way our alien considers how many miles he can travel with one gallon of gas. Since one serving of food contains a set number of calories, and the calories listed on the label are per serving, if you have multiple servings, you have to multiply the calories on the label by the number of servings to know the total calories you have eaten. For example, 232 calories times 10 servings equals 2320 total calories eaten. Rule 5: Know the type of fuel or calories you are burning. There are three main ingredients in food that provide energy: fats, carbohydrates, and proteins. If you add all three of these ingredients together, you can calculate the total calories. When our alien chooses his gas, he can choose from regular or premium grade. Both are still gas that his spaceship will use for energy but the concentrations are different. The same is true for food, only there are way more than two food choices. Rule 6: How fatty is your fuel? Fat is the most concentrated ingredient in food. This means that a large amount of calories are present in a small amount of fat. When choosing foods, select those with healthy fats like fish and nuts. But remember, a very little bit of fat burns for a long time and contains lots of energy. Rule 7: Sugar is too sweet for your tank. Carbohydrates are foods that are broken down into sugar by the body. If you are running a race that requires a lot of energy in a short period of time, carbohydrates are a good choice. However, if not burned right away, carbohydrates are stored almost immediately. Over time, diets high in process carbohydrates have been tied to many health problems. Choose healthier foods with less than 20 grams of carbohydrates per serving. Rule 8: Premium fuel for the body has a lot of protein. Protein is the third and final ingredient in food that provides us with calories. When the alien chooses high grade fuel, his spaceship run smoother for many years. Premium fuel for the body is high in protein. Protein provides good energy levels, keeps your weight steady, and allows the body to repair itself and stay healthy for many years. Rule 9: Food ingredients should not be in a foreign language. The ingredients found in the food you are eating are at the bottom of the nutritional label. Chances are, if you cannot pronounce the ingredients, that food is not a healthy option. Rule 10: Fuel with too many ingredients will clog your engine. Our alien knows that fuel with a long list of ingredients is likely processed and contains fillers and preservatives that may be unhealthy for his spaceship’s engine. In a similar way, choosing foods with less number of ingredients that you can pronounce is a good way to make sure the food you are about to eat is less processed.

What if I told you there was a food&nbsp;&nbsp; so nutrient dense that it could provide eight&nbsp; key nutrients and still fit into the palm of your&nbsp;&nbsp; hand? Well that's exactly what eggs have to offer&nbsp; and why I recommend them as part of a healthy and&nbsp;&nbsp; balanced diet. Eggs provide eight essential&nbsp; nutrients for human health and development.&nbsp;&nbsp; and here's how your body benefits. The&nbsp; human body needs a significant amount of&nbsp;&nbsp; protein each day as nearly every part of the&nbsp; body is either made from or uses protein for&nbsp;&nbsp; growth and repair. Protein-rich foods also&nbsp; help keep us feeling fuller for longer,&nbsp;&nbsp; making eggs a helpful ally in weight management.&nbsp; Egg yolks are one of the best sources of choline&nbsp;&nbsp; and just two to three eggs can provide nearly&nbsp; your entire daily amount of this nutrient. Choline&nbsp;&nbsp; supports healthy cell membranes, liver function&nbsp; and brain health. Egg yolks contain essential fats&nbsp;&nbsp; so if you're skipping the yolk when you eat eggs,&nbsp; you're actually forfeiting some key nutrients such&nbsp;&nbsp; as arachidonic acid and omega-3 fatty acids. These&nbsp; nutrient-dense components that reside in the yolk&nbsp;&nbsp; support immune function, have anti-inflammatory&nbsp; properties and promote the health of your eyes,&nbsp;&nbsp; brain and skin. Riboflavin, niacin pantothenic&nbsp; acid and biotin are all present in eggs. These&nbsp;&nbsp; b vitamins support both physical and mental health&nbsp; including nutrient absorption, energy metabolism&nbsp;&nbsp; and helping convert food into fuel. Vitamins a, d&nbsp; and e are all fat soluble meaning they need to be&nbsp;&nbsp; eaten with fat for the best absorption possible&nbsp; - just another reason to eat those egg yolks.&nbsp;&nbsp; Last but not least, selenium is a mineral&nbsp; that acts as an antioxidant in the body&nbsp;&nbsp; protecting cells from damage from things like&nbsp; environmental toxins and heavily processed foods.&nbsp;&nbsp; Selenium is also needed for healthy thyroid&nbsp; function so whether you like them scramble,&nbsp;&nbsp; hard boiled or over easy, eggs are a delicious&nbsp; way to ensure you get these eight key nutrients as&nbsp;&nbsp; part of your diet. When you choose Organic Valley&nbsp; eggs from hens that are free to forage outside and&nbsp;&nbsp; have a natural, omnivorous diet know that you're&nbsp; getting an egg that's raised without herbicides,&nbsp;&nbsp; pesticides or genetically modified organisms. The&nbsp; USDA organic seal ensures the highest standards of&nbsp;&nbsp; animal welfare for hens and an egg that delivers&nbsp; essential nutrition for you and your family.&nbsp;&nbsp; Keep on watching or visit the Organic Valley&nbsp; website for more nutritional information.

go ahead and soak it or boil it uh it will release some tannins it's not exessive but it does release tannin so again if you want to minimize that blow uh boil it or uh float it for a while to get some of those tannin out ahead of time it's a very cheap wood in terms of Price It's relatively inexpensive and so all the from all the wood that we've talked about today this is probably going to be on the cheaper end and so maybe you're somebody who's got primarily plants or rocks and you just want to add a little bit of wood this might be a really nice option for you it comes in lots of different sizes you can get them anywhere from around 4 in and then you've got the larger pieces like this and I've actually seen them much thicker and much wider where you could actually have some Nano cichlids use them as caves for potentially spawning so uh there is a wide variety of sizes now if there's a downside to the choya wood it does break down relatively rapidly I would say of all the wood types that we've talked about today the Cho wood probably breaks down the fastest and so this is not a wood that you're going to throw in your tank in five or six years from now expect it to still be there usually by that point it's broken down you might still have little chunks of the wood left over but it's probably not going to look like this there is one other type of wood that I want to mention in this video because from time to time it appears in some pet stores and it's really a wood that is more appropriate for a lizard setup than it is a fish tank and that is grape vine grap Vine is a very interesting wood and I think that's why people really are drawn to it they want to put that in an aquarium because it's got that nice twisty shape to it it's a very interesting pattern however it releases an excessive amount of organic materials I had The Misfortune of putting grape vine in an Aquascape at aquacell one time and the tank was absolutely destroyed it was extremely stressful because we put the grape vine in there not realizing that it was going to be such a big problem the next morning I had a whole bunch of fish that were all gasping for air at the top the tank was really really cloudy it wasn't tanon stained it was just had a white milky appearance I did an 80% water change I had to do that three times the next day just to keep those fish alive and then another two times the following day on that Sunday Grapevine is just not a wood that I would recommend putting in an aquarium even when you soak it it still releases a lot of organic compounds it takes available oxygen away from fish and it is a just a bad idea in general so if you see that at a pet store again I would just use that for maybe a palarum you might use that for lizards in a in a enclosure but not something I would necessarily put in an aquarium people always ask can you put driftwood from a lake or a river into your aquarium and my response always is risk versus reward there could be bacteria there could be pathogens on that wood that you will now be introducing into your aquarium that your fish you've never been exposed to that could be a potential nightmare so for me personally I don't take driftwood directly out of a lake or a river and put it in my aquarium I just don't think it's worth the risk there are lots of other types of woods out there not all of them are safe these are the most common most popular types of Driftwood that you can put in your aquarium would love to hear from you in the comments section below what are your favorites and if you want more information about how to care for driftwood and how to prep it check out that video in the upper right hand corner we talk about how to get that white fluffy stuff off of Driftwood and then the lower right hand corner how to prep it before you put it in your aquarium thank you so much for being here we'll see you in the next one

  15. @howdoesitgetbetter: The Egg Diet: Can You Really Lose Belly Fat in Just Two Weeks?  The Egg Diet promises significant weight loss in a short period, but it comes with risks. By creating a calorie deficit through a strict meal plan, individuals may lose weight quickly. However, the diet's low caloric intake can slow metabolism and lead to weight regain. A balanced approach with added calories and exercise is recommended for sustainable weight loss. The quest for quick weight loss solutions often leads people to extreme diets, and one such popular method is the Egg Diet. With claims of losing up to 20 pounds in just two weeks, many wonder if this diet is effective, safe, and worth trying. In this video, we will explore the details of the Egg Diet, its potential dangers, and how to approach weight loss more sustainably.

Understanding the Egg Diet
The Egg Diet is a low-calorie diet that primarily consists of eggs and a few other foods. It gained popularity after a video showcasing its effectiveness garnered over 20 million views. The premise is simple: if you consume fewer calories than you burn, you will lose weight. This fundamental principle of weight loss applies regardless of the specific diet you choose.

Daily Meal Breakdown

To illustrate how the Egg Diet works, let’s break down a typical day of eating:

Breakfast
Two eggs and tomatoes
Green tea
Total calories: Approximately 150 calories
This meal is light, but it sets the tone for the rest of the day.

Lunch
Four ounces of cod fish
Two hard-boiled egg whites
Frozen vegetables
Total calories: Approximately 300 calories
Lunch offers a bit more food, but it still remains low in calories.

Snack
One large apple
Total calories: Approximately 100 calories
A sweet treat to keep energy levels up.

Dinner
Four ounces of cottage cheese
One glass of low-fat milk
One piece of fried bread
Total calories: Approximately 250 calories

Dinner rounds out the day, but the total caloric intake remains low.

Total Caloric Intake
In total, the Egg Diet provides around 800 calories per day. For an average adult male who burns about 2,000 calories daily, this results in a 1,200 calorie deficit. Given that losing one pound of fat requires burning approximately 3,500 calories, this diet could theoretically lead to a weight loss of about 10 pounds in 30 days.

The Risks of the Egg Diet

While the prospect of rapid weight loss is appealing, the Egg Diet poses significant risks:

Nutritional Deficiency: Consuming only 800 calories is insufficient for most people, leading to potential deficiencies in essential nutrients.

Metabolic Slowdown: A drastic reduction in caloric intake can cause the body to adapt by slowing down metabolism, making it harder to lose weight over time.

Weight Regain: Once the diet ends, individuals may regain the weight lost, especially if they return to previous eating habits.

Recommendations for Sustainable Weight Loss
To achieve lasting weight loss, consider the following strategies:

Increase Caloric Intake: Aim for a more balanced diet that provides at least 1,300 to 1,500 calories per day, depending on your activity level.

Incorporate Exercise: Combine your diet with a workout program. Regular exercise not only burns calories but also helps maintain muscle mass during weight loss.

Track Your Steps: Aim for 10,000 steps per day to enhance your calorie-burning efforts.

Conclusion
The Egg Diet may offer a quick fix for weight loss, but its low caloric intake and potential health risks make it a less-than-ideal choice for sustainable weight management. Instead, focus on creating a calorie deficit through a balanced diet and regular exercise. By doing so, you can achieve your weight loss goals in a healthier and more sustainable way. If you're ready to take on this challenge, consider documenting your journey and sharing your progress with others for motivation and support.

  19. @vitya9011: Why Is Eating Eggs Not a Good Choice?
Cholesterol and Cardiovascular Disease
One egg contains approximately 186 mg of cholesterol, close to the recommended daily limit of 300 mg (per the American Heart Association). Studies show that regular egg consumption is associated with increased LDL ("bad") cholesterol levels, which heightens the risk of heart disease.

Risk of Type 2 Diabetes (T2D)
A 2019 meta-analysis found that consuming one egg per day increases the risk of developing type 2 diabetes by 27%, particularly in individuals already predisposed (e.g., those who are overweight).

Carcinogenic Compounds and Inflammation
Eggs contain choline, which is converted in the body into TMAO (trimethylamine-N-oxide). High levels of TMAO are linked to atherosclerosis and certain types of cancer, including prostate and colorectal cancer.

Food Safety and Antibiotic Resistance
Eggs are a leading cause of salmonella infections, contributing to approximately 78 million cases worldwide annually. Additionally, antibiotics used in industrial egg production drive the rise of antibiotic-resistant bacteria, a significant global health threat.

Environmental Impact
Producing just one egg requires around 200 liters of water. Industrial egg farming also contributes to greenhouse gas emissions, such as methane, exacerbating climate change and deforestation.

Animal Welfare Concerns
The egg industry kills billions of male chicks each year shortly after hatching because they are not economically viable. Even in "free-range" systems, hens endure harsh living conditions and are often subjected to painful practices like beak trimming.

Conclusion
Egg consumption negatively impacts health, the environment, and animal welfare. A plant-based diet can entirely avoid these issues while providing all necessary nutrients, including protein and B vitamins, from cruelty-free, sustainable sources.
